\begin{document}
\thispagestyle{plain}
{\sffamily\small\bfseries\color{accent}URIEL RESEARCH\hfill 23 NOVEMBER 2026}\par\vspace{13pt}
{\Huge\bfseries\color{ink}Cofinal endpoints and the prime sum\par}\vspace{7pt}
{\Large\color{mute}How much of an arithmetic window is certified by one witness, and how much can two readings recover?\par}\vspace{14pt}
\textbf{Abstract.} A localized analytic argument tests a character sum on intervals that can be chosen arbitrarily far out. We trace the arithmetic data behind these intervals to ask whether the same sum has enough certified endpoints to control missing portions. For every sufficiently large outer scale, one choice of a prime pair and a fixed page function supplies genuine local data throughout a larger interval than the published window uses. For each fixed \(1<\lambda<2\) the same level's prime sum has cofinal power-\(\lambda\) windows. For the conductor-one principal character we reach \(\lambda=2\) exactly. An upper bound discarded in the published host construction forces the rescaling factor to be one. The supplementary formal argument runs from named original arithmetic and global inputs to the actual normalized zeta prime sum. A separate written reconstruction retains the identity of particular qualified ordinates through the global design.

Two endpoints carrying an arithmetic test can certify an entire interval if they use the same exceptional sets and pass a strict crossover in their cutoffs. Two values of the prime sum can instead admit an interior excursion. We derive a sharp worst-case gap for the outer-scale supply, and independently extend scalar bounds a growing distance using a uniform slope estimate. On the actual corrected character modulo four, both boundaries of \([256,512]\) pass a square-root ceiling that the interior exceeds. At a more generous ceiling two boundaries and 23 specific interior tests certify every point, while 33 evenly spaced passing tests still leave room for excess. These results quantify the difference between finding good intervals arbitrarily late and bridging a gap around a prescribed point.

\medskip\textbf{Evidence.} The package contains original-source anchors, supplementary Lean declarations and replay receipts, pre-recorded primary protocols, deliberately failing controls, exact rational tests, and directed numerical enclosures. The manuscript and its illustrations compile from one editable source.
\clearpage\tableofcontents
\medskip\subsection*{Reading the question}
Imagine measuring a curve at both ends of a bridge. Two measured heights leave room for something higher between them. If each instrument instead certifies a list of integer obstructions, the answer depends on how the list changes as one walks across. Here each obstruction enters the test once and its allowable size grows with position. When the left test already covers every integer that could be too large at the right, passing both tests settles the whole bridge. The arithmetic and numerical experiments ask which kind of reading we possess at each stage of the proof.
\section{The question inherited from the preceding experiments}
\subsection{One observable and three meanings of an endpoint}
Let \(F\) denote the positive character family attached to a fixed analytic level. Its weighted real smoothed sum is \(G_F\). To use the localized analytic criterion at a line \(\theta>1/2\), one may supply nonnegative majorants on arbitrarily late intervals \([Y,Y^{1+\delta}]\), with upper mass
\[
 \int_Y^{Y^{1+\delta}} R(u)u^{-\theta-1}\,du\le Y^\eta
 \qquad\text{for each }\eta>0 .
\]
The start may depend on the requested lateness and the loss \(\eta\). The family appearing on the left must stay the same in a given instance of the criterion. A hypothetical zero to the right creates a lower-mass obstruction on every sufficiently late interval at a suitable intermediate exponent. The previous study examined the loss of mass control in holes created by an arithmetic test. Its chord inequality assumed the endpoints of the missing component already satisfy a bound on the same observable. The present study examines that assumption. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/CharFamily.lean#L41-L45}{[Original analytic window predicate]}\quad\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/pull/16}{[Hypothetical zero and arithmetic windows]}\quad\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/pull/17}{[Exceptional bands and positive mass]}

At a point of an already selected window the source provides detailed local data. At the boundary of a proposed obstruction one might know that a particular exceptional integer has become harmless. A third possibility is a numerical observation that the prime sum lies below a given ceiling. The first can feed the arithmetic comparison. The second settles only the specified integer until all the other requirements have been checked. The third can feed an analytic interpolation inequality with its own error. Recording all three as a single true-or-false observation would change the problem.

There are also two levels of family identity. A character family fixes the observable. A family of arithmetic data supplies a domain and estimates controlling that observable. The final scalar can be the same even where the local witnesses differ. Yet its threshold can depend on the entire arithmetic family. Rechoosing that family at each point could put the threshold past the point one just selected. A proof that first fixes the family and its threshold, then ranges over late points of its domain, avoids this reversal.

\subsection{Predictions and possible failures}
\begin{enumerate}
\item If the host remembers every ordinate possessing the required local datum, the fixed construction should propagate more than its displayed interval. An existential record that forgets the relation between the chosen host and the full set would require us to reconstruct its witnesses.
\item If a common exception list must pass at both boundaries, there should be a calculable distinction between a hole that can hide an integer and a hole that cannot. A strict crossover should have a boundary counterexample. Tests involving different lists should allow failure.
\item If the scalar question admits the same inference from two readings, a true positive interior excess between passing endpoints would falsify it. A better placement of observations should reduce the cost of the adverse curvature. Its optimal location may depend on whether one sees a measured height or just a pass.
\item If a fixed family has bounded slope, a power margin at a certified point should buy a growing neighborhood outside its arithmetic domain. Its relative width should still shrink. The tail proof and finite slope prefix are separate obligations.
\end{enumerate}
The worst empty logarithmic interval is a follow-up deduction from the recovered support, independently reviewed and then tested. An optimal bound for an abstract observation class is sharp with respect to the information retained in that class. Independent examples using the true prime function address which compatible possibility occurs at a finite scale.

\subsection{The quantities that are held fixed}
The outer variable \(X\) bounds the ambient arithmetic problem. The selector returns a start \(W\) in the unscaled variable, a prime pair, and information across \([W,W^2]\). A fixed change of scale uses \(x=ku\), with \(k\ge1\). In the field section \(k\) is reused locally for a summation integer, as indicated there. Read it as \(m\) when it appears inside an exceptional set. The field cutoff exponent \(C_t\), the tolerance \(\varepsilon\), and the search parameter \(J\) play different roles. In the numerical section \(C\) is simply the coefficient in a ceiling \(C\sqrt y\).

A set of windows is cofinal when, for each starting threshold, at least one whole window begins beyond it. Even very long cofinal windows alone say little about the ones that are missed. Here the selector must answer \emph{every sufficiently large outer query}, even though it may choose the inner window. That extra universal quantifier powers the gap calculation. Log coordinates reveal its content; the finite character experiment works in ordinary coordinates so the slope jumps at prime powers remain visible.


\section{From a cohomological estimate to a qualified ordinate}
\label{sec:transport-raw}

Fix a primitive Dirichlet character \(\psi\) throughout this section. The level \(\Lambda=\mathrm{cyc}(\psi)\) fixes a finite list of characters and their weights. Its real-valued function \(\Lambda.G_m\) equals \(G_F\) for one fixed analytic character family \(F\), at every real argument. In particular, the phrase ``the same prime sum'' has an exact meaning even when the number fields later used to bound it change. In the construction below the conductor of \(\psi\), the level and \(F\) remain fixed. The auxiliary primes, page number, host, retained set and row index can vary with the argument. The fixed object on the analytic side is what lets an estimate at one endpoint constrain the same curve measured at another.
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/PrimitiveCosets/LevelFamily.lean#L27-L52}{[Level to analytic family]}

\subsection{A pointwise interface with six obligations}

For \(0<\epsilon<1/52\), a scale \(x\) qualifies at constants \(J,c\) if there exist a real \(Y\) and integers \(q,d,n\) satisfying the six fields of \texttt{WinDatum}. The first two compare the pair scale \(Y\) with the measured scale \(x\). The third asks for a good prime pair associated to this range. Two more prescribe the order of the frame, to within a factor of two. The remaining field controls an arithmetic quantity for all compatible number fields. In formulas the five geometric, pair and page fields are
\[
 x^{1/J}\le Y,\qquad Y\le x,\qquad
 \mathrm{PairGood}(\psi.N,\epsilon,Y,x,q,d),\qquad
 x^{2+8\epsilon}\le c\,2^n\le2x^{2+8\epsilon}.
\]
For each \(E\) satisfying \(\mathrm{IsChiField}(\psi,n,q,d,E)\) the sixth field requires
\[
 \mathrm{VVle}\!\left(E,\left\lfloor x^{400}\right\rfloor,
       \sqrt{\exp(x^\epsilon)}\right).
\]
The universal quantifier matters. The proof constructs the number field of its own host later and specializes the estimate to that field. A calculation for one field needs an identification with that later choice, or a theorem that gives the estimate uniformly for the whole indicated class. The definition of \(\mathrm{PairGood}\) contributes further content. Its twist estimates apply at every ordinate within its own specified range, and its zero boxes apply to all indicated twists. Thus the endpoint argument below can skip the interior of a cohomological band while continuing to require the original prime-pair hypotheses over their stated domains.
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/TwistForm.lean#L19-L26}{[Six fields]}
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/PairGood.lean#L20-L34}{[Prime-pair conditions]}

Put \(a=2+8\epsilon\) and \(k=c^{1/a}\) for the positive constant \(c\ge1\) from \texttt{fieldChi}. Then \(k\ge1\). Write \(x=ku\) and use a page \(n\) satisfying \(u^a\le2^n\le 2u^a\). Multiplying by \(c\) gives the two frame-order inequalities at \(x\). The original field estimate at \(u\) uses the integer cutoff \(\lfloor u^{401}\rfloor\). The target estimate at \(x\) uses \(\lfloor(ku)^{400}\rfloor\). For \(u>0\) the underlying real inequality is equivalent to \(k^{400}\le u\). Taking integer floors preserves the weak inequality. The size allowance also moves in the favorable direction because \(\epsilon>0\), \(u\le ku\), and the square root and exponential are increasing. The source lemma \texttt{VVle.mono} performs both weakenings together.

The new kernel-checked lemma \texttt{localDatum} implements these steps with the original definitions. Given the range conditions, an original pair certificate up to \(X\ge x\), the appropriate page and \(\mathrm{VAt}(\psi,\epsilon,401,q,d,n,u)\), it constructs the original \texttt{WinDatum} at \(ku\). Monotonicity in the upper range of the prime-pair certificate permits evaluation at \(x\). This proof takes no interval of valid cohomological inputs surrounding \(u\). Consequently, any improvement of the exceptional-weight bound that yields the full \(\mathrm{VAt}\) at \(u\) can enter at that line. To get two endpoints one may apply this same statement twice at different pages or with different pairs, provided both endpoint certificates use the same \(J,c,\psi,\epsilon\) for the common construction below. A field bound at a point with an unsuitable page or pair cannot discharge a different obligation by numerical coincidence.
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/ExistsPageN.lean#L39-L62}{[Point and window predicates]}
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/WinPairs.lean#L56-L81}{[Original pointwise adapter]}

\subsection{The interval the original estimates already pay for}

The fixed page schedule \(e(u)\) is chosen before \(J\). The arithmetic window hypothesis gives \(J\ge1\) and an outer threshold \(X_0\) for that schedule. The prime-pair theorem gives \(Y_1\) such that each \(Y\ge Y_1\) admits a pair good through \(Y^J\). One sufficient outer threshold, with this order of choices, is
\[
 M=\max(1,Y_1,k^{400}),\qquad T=\max(X_0,M^J).
\]
For every \(X\ge T\), set \(Y=X^{1/J}\). Choose a pair \(q,d\) supplied for this \(Y\). It qualifies in the cohomological hypothesis for the very same \(X\). Hence there is a \(W\) such that
\[
       X^{1/J}\le W,\qquad W^2\le X,\qquad
       \mathrm{VAt}(\psi,\epsilon,401,q,d,e(u),u)
                         \quad(W\le u\le W^2).
\]
The final fact is uniform in \(u\) after this pair and \(W\) have been chosen. It may hold at further points as well. This order of choices gives a single \(J,k,T\) for arbitrarily many outer queries, even though no algorithm for their values is provided by these declarations.

The new \texttt{rawSupply} conclusion on the measured axis is
\begin{equation}
 kW\le x\le\min(X,kW^2)
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \mathrm{WinDatum}\bigl(\psi,\epsilon,J,c,x,X^{1/J},q,d,e(x/k)\bigr).
 \label{eq:transport-exact-domain}
\end{equation}
Indeed the lower bound gives \(u=x/k\ge W\ge k^{400}\). The upper bound separates into \(x\le X\), where the pair certificate is available, and \(u\le W^2\), where the field estimate is available. Also \(W\ge X^{1/J}\) gives \(Y\le x\); monotonicity of the \(1/J\) power gives \(x^{1/J}\le Y\). All six fields then follow from the pointwise adapter. Equation~\eqref{eq:transport-exact-domain} is a guaranteed intersection of two domains of applicability. Pointwise arithmetic estimates may continue farther on either side if additional input is supplied.

There is a visible quantitative consequence even with no new information about exceptional centers. Since \(W^2\le X\) and \(k\ge1\), the right edge in \eqref{eq:transport-exact-domain} is at least \(W^2\). For \(1<\lambda<2\), the condition for fitting a power window of base \(kW\) into this guaranteed part is exactly
\begin{equation}
       (kW)^\lambda\le W^2
       \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
       W\ge k^{\lambda/(2-\lambda)} .
       \label{eq:transport-width}
\end{equation}
This follows by division by \(W^\lambda>0\) and taking the positive \((2-\lambda)\)-th root. \texttt{widthRoom} kernel-checks the sufficient direction used in the proof. Request \(X\) sufficiently large for \(X^{1/J}\) to exceed the right side. The unboundedness of outer scales permits this for each fixed \(\lambda<2\). It also allows the new base \(kW\) to exceed any requested lower limit. The theorem \texttt{winPairsBelowTwo} kernel-checks the resulting cofinal windows for the original \texttt{WinDatum}, from the original \(\mathrm{VWinAt}(\psi,401)\) and \texttt{PairEx} hypotheses. The proof can fix \(J\) before successively trying different widths by using \texttt{rawSupply}. The threshold for a particular width can depend on that width.

For orientation take \(k=2,W=2^{400},X=2^{800}\) as an exact geometry control. Measured in base-two logarithms, the base \(kW\) is \(401\), its published power-\(3/2\) endpoint is \(601.5\), and the right edge of the guaranteed intersection is \(800\). A power-two window would require \(802\). The rows of the numerical test record these exponents exactly as integers or fractions. They impose no claim that the selected arithmetic constants have these numerical values. For growing \(W\) the largest power directly fitting under \(W^2\) is \(2\log W/(\log W+\log k)\), tending to two from below when \(k>1\). At \(k=1\) the geometric obstruction vanishes. For \(k>1\) and \(X=W^2\), one such input block cannot supply the entire power-two window through this adapter. The distinction between approaching an exponent and attaining it persists at every finite scale in that control. It does not preclude a different arithmetic argument giving a power-two interval.

\subsection{The principal character attains the square}

The cutoff \(\lambda<2\) in the last paragraph has a precise arithmetic exception. For the primitive principal character \(\psi=\mathbf 1\) of conductor one, the scale multiplier of the field construction is exactly one. The headline version of \texttt{fieldChi} retains \(1\le c\), but its first proof step obtains the stronger statement
\[
                   1\le c\le \#\langle\psi.\chi\rangle .
\]
In \texttt{ordAny} the upper bound comes from the order of a subgroup of a fixed finite group. The eventual-order coefficient \(c\) is chosen as an attained minimum of a nonincreasing sequence of positive integer subgroup orders. This argument supplies an existential stabilization page \(e_0\); it gives no need to compute that page in order to read the bound on the stabilized value. For the identity character its subgroup of integral powers is the trivial subgroup. Its cardinality is one, which forces \(c=1\) for the very same field witness. The source-derived declaration \texttt{fieldChiWithCap} retains the dropped conjunct through the complete original field-construction body. The new \texttt{principalCap} checks the specialization on the original primitive-character type.
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/OrdAny.lean#L86-L107}{[Stabilized order and its cap]}
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/FieldChi.lean#L20-L30}{[Order coefficient used by the same field witness]}

It follows that \(k^{2+8\epsilon}=1\) with \(k\ge1\), hence \(k=1\). The two upper constraints in \eqref{eq:transport-exact-domain} become \(x\le X\) and \(x\le W^2\); the second implies the first. The entire interval \([W,W^2]\) qualifies. There is also no ambiguity in the analytic observable for this character. Its cyclic level retains only \(\mathbf 1\), so erasing that principal entry leaves an empty nonprincipal sum. The level's \(G_m\) equals the original centered zeta ramp, denoted \texttt{Deduction.G} in the archive. This is equality of the whole real functions. The new \texttt{principalLevel} proves it for every \(y\) using the original level membership and zeta-ramp identifications.
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Formalism/SharpRestriction/RamificationFrobenius/TotientGaloisClasses/ExplicitFormulaErrorOfHostConditions.lean#L102-L125}{[Level formed from a subgroup]}
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/PrimitiveCosets/GzEq.lean#L6-L12}{[Zeta-ramp identity]}

Consequently \texttt{principalScalar} composes the strengthened host construction with the fixed-level theorem. Given the original \texttt{TopG}, \texttt{ShortNorm}, \texttt{SmallOne} and \(\mathrm{VWinAt}(\mathbf1,401)\) inputs, for each \(\delta>0\) there are arbitrarily late \(Z\) for which
\[
             G_{\zeta}(x)\le x^{1/2+\delta}\qquad (Z\le x\le Z^2).
\]
The tolerance and all comparison thresholds are fixed before requesting the late interval. The location \(Z\) still comes from existence, may depend on \(\delta\) and the requested lower limit, and need not coincide with a separately studied band. The width exponent two is attained in the squared cohomological interface for this specific character. This leaves open whether still wider windows follow from other inputs or for other characters. The specialization is a gain over the generic rescaling argument precisely where the group-theoretic cap eliminates its geometric loss.

\section{Which qualified scales survive the family construction?}
\label{sec:transport-mark}

\subsection{Two conclusions with different verification requirements}

There are two ways to use a collection of qualified ordinates. To obtain the existence of cofinal scalar windows of a fixed width, package the host supply at precisely that width. To obtain scalar values at \emph{identified} extra points, retain a relation between the raw support and the support of the final family. The published type of the host theorem makes that distinction consequential. \texttt{HostExW} asserts existence of a scale system \(S\) with arbitrarily late complete windows of exponent \(\lambda\), and a host for every sufficiently large scale in \(S\). It provides neither uniqueness of \(S\) nor a means to identify a scale known separately to be in another eligible system.

For \(\lambda<2\), the first conclusion uses a short source reconstruction. In \texttt{supplyOfAdmOf} replace the published \texttt{winPairs} input at exponent \(3/2\) with \texttt{winPairsBelowTwo}. Construct \(S\) with the original \(J\) and the set
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal Q_\epsilon=\{x:\ \exists\,Y,q,d,n,\ 
             \mathrm{WinDatum}(\psi,\epsilon,J,c,x,Y,q,d,n)\}.
 \label{eq:transport-qual}
\end{equation}
Its complete-window field now uses \(\lambda\). The remainder of the host proof takes an arbitrary \(s\) with \(s.J=J\) and \(s.x\in\mathcal Q_\epsilon\) and uses its \texttt{WinDatum}. None of the local calculations use \(\lambda\). In particular the field construction produces a frame and a host with the stated page and pair. The rank choice uses the two frame-order bounds from that very datum. The final retained set supplies the explicit-formula data, while the universal field estimate is instantiated at the field of that host and weakened to the selected rank. Host data at a neighboring scale play no role in this substitution.
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/ShortNorms/supplyOfAdmOf.lean#L48-L63}{[Comprehension and local membership]}
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/ShortNorms/supplyOfAdmOf.lean#L93-L135}{[Field, rank, retained set and specialization]}

Two small wrappers, \texttt{supplyOfAdm} and \texttt{supplyChi}, fix \(3/2\) in their declared result types. Their supplied host and admissible-rank facts are independent of the exponent; instantiate the reconstructed host theorem with them. At the other end the published \texttt{TopPlug} also passes \(3/2\) explicitly. The function it obtains from the global shape and the hypotheses already has type \texttt{TopG}. This function accepts \emph{every} \(\lambda>1\) given a host supply at that \(\lambda\). Apply it to the reconstructed supply. It returns cofinal windows of the specified width in one connected family at each sufficiently small positive \(\epsilon\). The unchanged level comparison gives \(G_F(x)\le x^{1/2+9\epsilon}\) at its sufficiently late window points, using the exhibited global choices \(e_D=2,e_L=0\). The supplementary \texttt{scalarBelowTwo} performs the composition as a Lean theorem from the original global interface \texttt{TopG}, the short norm-prime input, the auxiliary-prime input and the window hypothesis for this character. It uses the modified original host proof, including the original admissible-rank theorem. The original project supplies the two prime inputs in \texttt{ShortNorms/Primes}; the full build of all original results furnishing these four premises was outside this run. Existence of the returned family alone gives no locator for a specified endpoint obtained in a separate invocation of the raw construction.
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/ThetaCorrespondence/ThetaPairLifts/ThirdPlaceSeries/TopPlug.lean#L12-L18}{[Published specialization]}
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/ZeroFreeAllOfTopG.lean#L12-L33}{[Width-generic global interface]}

\subsection{A marked version of the design argument}

The comprehension in \eqref{eq:transport-qual} already includes isolated points. Adding two points whose data meet its six conditions is literally a membership proof. No interval joining them is required. What remains is to transmit that membership to a family supplying the final chain of inequalities. We audited the concrete witness-producing route, including the changes of records made along it. The supplementary \texttt{supplyWithSupport} strengthens the original host construction by exposing the exact equality \(S.\mathrm{Win}=\mathcal Q_\epsilon\) for the very \(S\) carrying the hosts. The remaining proposed strengthening decorates the later design-family existence statement with a property of the witness it produces. It cannot be obtained by projecting extra information from the published existential result.

For each eligible \(\epsilon\), choose \(J,c\) and the raw system once. This can be done by classical choice with \(\psi\) fixed; the choices in \texttt{supplyOfAdmOf} precede the later design conditions and the queried endpoints. Use
\[
           \mathcal M_\epsilon(A)\ \equiv\
                        \mathcal Q_\epsilon\subseteq A.\mathrm{Win}
\]
as the family property to be retained. Equality of supports is available at construction and suffices for inclusion. For the host and kit stages the parallel property says \(\mathcal Q_\epsilon\subseteq S.\mathrm{Win}\) about their scale system. Witnesses for which the exponent or \(J\) differs need their own proof of membership; a change in bookkeeping cannot transfer an unrelated certificate.

First, \texttt{hostQ} obtains \(S\) before choosing the adjusted prime data and returns exactly this \(S\). The more demanding \texttt{hostWW} forms a family of temporary kits on qualifying scales to solve a weight requirement. The constructor \texttt{exists\_famP\_of\_kits} supplies both \(A.J=S.J\) and \(A.\mathrm{Win}=S.\mathrm{Win}\). \texttt{hostWW} explicitly rewrites by the latter equality to find an index at the requested scale. It uses equality of the argument, of \(\epsilon\) and of \(J\) to transport the weighted result to the input scale. Its output again uses \(S\). Lower thresholds increase to accommodate the required conditions; the underlying support stays available to every sufficiently late query.
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/HostWW.lean#L73-L87}{[Retrieving the same scale]}

Next, \texttt{kitS} combines the host and object constraints, takes maxima of thresholds and returns the same \(S\). In \texttt{designS} a kit is chosen for every sufficiently late member. The family constructor writes \(S.\mathrm{Win}\) directly into the family record. It uses a fallback scale to define auxiliary objects at arguments outside the good region; the index subtype at a late ordinate requires membership in \(S.\mathrm{Win}\), so those fallback objects cannot be queried there as good data. The constructor explicitly returns the support equality. \texttt{designS} uses that equality to transfer the cofinal-window field, then changes the returned family with \texttt{setTu}. The definition of this update changes only the real parameter \(\mathrm{CTu}\). Thus the marked predicate transfers by definitional equality across this last step. It is the identity of the support that this argument preserves. Changing the value of \(\mathrm{CTu}\) changes the record.
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AbelianVarietyTheory/Proofs/GlobalInterpolation/ExistsFamPOfKits.lean#L159-L170}{[Writing the family support]}
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/DesignS.lean#L89-L96}{[Final family in the design]}
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AbelianVarietyTheory/Definitions/GlobalTwisting/AuxiliaryPrimeMemAvoidedPrimes.lean#L145-L152}{[The record update]}

There is one more distinction at the class level. The original object-selection argument fixes a class \(\mathcal A\), its selection and its duties. The choice of \(\mathcal A\) is shared by numerous universal closure results. It would be a substantial additional assumption to claim all those results for an arbitrary newly selected class. A more economical modification holds \(\mathcal A\) fixed, reconstructs its existential design argument with the marked result, and runs the global end on the subclass \(\mathcal A(A)\wedge G.\mathrm{cls}(\lambda,A)\wedge\mathcal M_\epsilon(A)\). The selection and duties on this subclass are obtained by projecting to \(\mathcal A\). Universal closure statements restrict along the same projection. The published \texttt{TopShape} already uses this method to add the character requirement \(G.\mathrm{cls}\), before taking the common final family. The extra conjunct can be put in the class argument of the working-to-frozen conversion, \texttt{designChoices\_upN}, and \texttt{designExists\_of\_choices}. The latter requests a family at a strengthened design requirement \(K\sqcup K'\) and returns that very family, splitting the large-parameter proof. Finally \texttt{topDesWin} returns the family taken at the joint closure requirement \(K\), with the marked conjunct obtained alongside its character condition. This describes the modified witnesses and the required subclass restrictions. This full decorated proof remains a source-audited reconstruction.
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/ThetaCorrespondence/ThetaPairLifts/ThirdPlaceSeries/TopShape.lean#L47-L55}{[Restriction of global closure facts]}
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/DesWin.lean#L22-L31}{[One final connected family]}

\subsection{Reading off two bounds and auditing failures}

The supplement kernel-checks the final step against the original level-comparison theorem. If \(A\) is one family at positive \(\epsilon\) with its explicit-formula certificate, its window chain and \(\mathcal Q_\epsilon\subseteq A.\mathrm{Win}\), then there is a single \(t_*\) such that
\[
 x\ge t_*,\quad x\in\mathcal Q_\epsilon
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 G_F(x)\le x^{1/2+(e_D+e_L+7)\epsilon}.
\]
Internally the final comparison uses the maximum of three thresholds, for the scalar arithmetic core, the family chain, and the explicit formula. At each \(x\) the chain selects an index \(i\) carrying the quantitative core. The explicit-formula condition applies to every index of the family there and can be evaluated at this same \(i\). The retained-set identification is internal to this specialization. At two different \(x\) the indices may differ. Both computations return bounds on the same \(G_F\), so no assertion equating the two local hosts has to be proved. More generally, two independently certified scalar bounds using the same fixed \(F\) and exponent can be combined even if their proof families differ, provided each endpoint exceeds its own valid threshold. Our marked construction buys one threshold for an entire set.
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/PrimitiveCosets/WindowInequalityOfArtinInputs.lean#L17-L25}{[Three thresholds and the common local index]}

A threshold is part of the information content. Choosing a new \(S\) after each requested endpoint could yield a new \(t_*\) above that endpoint every time. For the elementary control with marked points \(2n,2n+1\) and cutoff \(2n+2\), a scalar property can fail at both marked points and hold everywhere past the cutoff. This remains possible for every \(n\) if a new scalar property is chosen in each run; a single fixed property that fails at all the marked points also makes the late implication on each finite marked set vacuous. On a fixed cofinal collection of qualified points with a fixed cutoff the obstruction disappears. A cofinal collection of \emph{pairs} requires the smaller endpoint to become arbitrarily large. The outer-scale supply above meets this condition on its exhibited blocks, since \(W\ge X^{1/J}\) with fixed positive \(J\). Arbitrarily prescribed endpoints outside those blocks require their own qualification certificates.

The finite control enumerates four ordered scales, every possible raw support, final support and set of scalar failures, and five choices for the lower cutoff. Among \(20{,}480\) configurations it retains \(4{,}651\) satisfying containment of the final support in the raw set and success at every late final-support point. Of these, \(1{,}275\) still admit a late failure in the raw set. The smallest example displayed in the receipt takes two raw points and retains only the first; a failure at the second violates no premise. Equality of supports gives \(781\) surviving configurations and zero failures, while testing all proper marked subsets of the final support gives \(7{,}845\) subset instances and zero violations. These are checks on the logic of the interface. Each configuration is weighted once, with no intended probability model over arithmetic families. The separate dyadic grid makes \(525\) exact probes of the rescaling endpoints against the conjunction of their two domain inequalities. The grid includes equality and the immediately adjacent excluded arguments.

For a genuine band whose original screen fails inside, the missing certificate can now be described locally. Fix the character and small tolerance, fix the generator before the band, show the relevant even and odd layer estimates including all other active weights at the proposed preimages, and verify the remaining pair, page and field hypotheses of \texttt{localDatum}. A cofinal family of such pairs must meet these tests under a common set of constants. The source calculation supplies whole blocks by avoiding the obstructing configurations and can certify many points beyond its displayed power-\(3/2\) subwindow. It does not identify a particular exceptional integer, give positions of its centers, or establish that an exhibited exterior point crosses a genuine failure of the original full arithmetic criterion. Bounds on the scalar prime sum can sometimes be obtained without deciding that membership. The remaining experiment on crossing an actual failed band requires distinguishing those two kinds of information at the same fixed character and scales.

\subsection{Forcing a scalar window into a prescribed union of outer portions}

There is an intermediate result between knowing an unspecified window exists and knowing the value of the scalar at every qualified point. It helps distinguish which conclusions genuinely need the marked family theorem. Fix a target width \(1<\nu<4/3\). Choose \(\beta\) with \(3/2<\beta<2/\nu\). At a fixed small \(\epsilon\), use the uniform outer supply to choose increasingly separated \(W_j\) tending to infinity. One may choose each new lower bound beyond the previous right endpoint because \(W\ge X^{1/J}\). Discard a finite initial segment so that
\[
      (kW_j)^{3/2} < W_j^\beta,\qquad
      (W_j^\beta)^\nu \le W_j^2 .
\]
The intervals \([W_j^\beta,W_j^2]\) lie strictly to the right of the endpoint of the power-\(3/2\) subwindow belonging to the same choice of \(W_j\). Each interval is contained in \(\mathcal Q_\epsilon\) by \eqref{eq:transport-exact-domain}. Let \(D_\epsilon\) be their union. It contains complete cofinal power-\(\nu\) windows. The choices of unions can be fixed as a function of \(\epsilon\) before applying the global theorem. The positions are specified by the chosen arithmetic witnesses and remain nonnumerical.

Restrict the scale system in the local host proof to \(D_\epsilon\). Its original host implication is available there by inclusion in \(\mathcal Q_\epsilon\). Use the point requirement
\[
              G'(s,\ldots)=G_\psi(s,\ldots)\ \wedge\ s.x\in D_{s.\epsilon}.
\]
The additional conjunct is immediate for the chosen input system. The original width-generic \texttt{TopG} can be applied with \(G'\), since the character part implies its \(\mathrm{HypV}\) premise. The resulting family has a point of its index type at each late window member. At such an index \(A.L\) has the queried ordinate and tolerance, so its \(G'\) certificate places the original scalar ordinate in \(D_\epsilon\). Every sufficiently late output window is therefore contained in this union. The prescribed gaps between consecutive outer portions ensure that each entire connected output interval lies in a single one of them. Projection to the character requirement supplies the usual level comparison on that interval.

This is a source-level deduction using an explicit restriction of the local supply and an ordinary invocation of the global theorem. Domain marking in a \emph{point requirement} forces the returned domain to be a subset of a designated region. It suffices to obtain some scalar windows in the exhibited exterior portions. For scalar bounds at both preassigned endpoints of an individual hole we need containment in the opposite direction, or direct scalar certificates at those endpoints. In particular the region just constructed can lie inside other possible good arithmetic windows; it supplies no example of a classified exceptional integer between the endpoints.


\paragraph{A fixed character all the way to the scalar bound.}
Let $\psi$ be fixed, let $\lambda>1$, and suppose that the original \texttt{TopG} and the character-specific host-existence premise \texttt{HostExW} at this same width are supplied. For every fixed $\delta>0$ and every $B$, the argument gives a $Y\geq \max(B,1)$ such that
\[
    (\operatorname{Level.cyc}\psi).\operatorname{Gm}(x)
       \leq x^{1/2+\delta}\qquad (Y\leq x\leq Y^{\lambda}).
\]
The observable in this assertion is the one defined by the comparison proof. For general $\psi$ it contains the zeta term and a weighted sum of real parts of character terms at its cyclic level. A separate identity is needed to identify it with a simpler scalar quantity in a specialization. The statement supplies a complete window of the original width, so at width two it bounds the observable both at $Y$ and at $Y^2$ with the same tolerance.

To extract this conclusion, apply \texttt{TopG} to the request \texttt{GChi} for this character. Its connected-family output chooses constants $e_D,e_L$ and $\epsilon_1>0$ before the family tolerance. Choose $0<\epsilon<\epsilon_1$ such that $(e_D+e_L+7)\epsilon\leq\delta$. The original small-tolerance lemma permits either sign of the coefficient. The selected family has windows of width $\lambda$, the explicit-formula input for the fixed level, and the tied estimate on its family scales. The original level comparison gives the exponent $1/2+(e_D+e_L+7)\epsilon$ beyond a family-specific lower threshold. Intersecting with the tail beyond that threshold and one preserves cofinal windows. Monotonicity of $x^s$ in $s$ there gives the displayed estimate. This recovers a scalar assertion before the analytic zero-free criterion is invoked. Its witness $Y$ may depend on $\delta$ and on the comparison family. It makes no claim that particular endpoint marks supplied earlier remain present in the selected family. A claim about arbitrarily prescribed marks would require a stronger comparison result.


\subsection{Three different statements about where a window lies}
Let \(Q_\varepsilon\) be the set of all scales qualifying for the local data under fixed parameters. Let \(A_\varepsilon\) be the scale set of an arithmetic family whose estimates are tied to the chosen level. A proven inclusion \(Q_\varepsilon\subseteq A_\varepsilon\) lets us ask about \emph{particular} late members of \(Q_\varepsilon\), including two we identified before forming a host. A result giving only that \(A_\varepsilon\) contains some arbitrarily late windows of a specified width is adequate for the analytic contradiction, even if it forgets the relation to those members. Conversely, imposing an additional point requirement on a newly constructed family may give \(A_\varepsilon\subseteq D_\varepsilon\) for a preferred region \(D_\varepsilon\). That places the whole returned interval in \(D_\varepsilon\); it does not let us prescribe which two points of \(D_\varepsilon\) are returned.

This distinction is visible in the order of the quantifiers. With a host carrying a prescribed width \(\lambda>1\) for one character and the source comparison available at that width, a typical consequence has the form
\[
 \forall\delta>0\ \forall T\ \exists Y\ge T\ \forall y\in[Y,Y^\lambda],
 \qquad G_F(y)\le y^{1/2+\delta}.
\]
We can pick \(T\), shrink \(\delta\), or specify \(\lambda\) in the appropriate allowed range before requesting the window. The result tells us neither that every \(Y\ge T\) works nor that the same \(Y\) works for every \(\delta\). The character observable stays fixed; the chosen internal tolerance, family of hosts and window may depend on \(\delta\). Taking \(\delta=0\) by passing to a limit of distinct windows would require compactness or uniformity that is absent here. The finite square-root comparisons in the numerical section are direct computations at that exponent and therefore have a different justification.

Finally, the original assembly makes clear where the remaining large mathematical inputs enter. Its short-norm and small-split-prime results are named declarations, and the published top-level call obtains its global comparison from the Green and class hypotheses before substituting the original \(3/2\) host supply. The study reuses these interfaces while retaining the width or the identity of an endpoint. Reading the assembly at the pin tells us how the ingredients are used. Replaying a supplementary implication with such an ingredient as a premise does not independently recheck the full proof of that ingredient. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/ShortNorms/Primes.lean#L5-L7}{[Original short-norm and prime inputs]}\quad\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/ThetaCorrespondence/ThetaPairLifts/ThirdPlaceSeries/TopPlug.lean#L12-L16}{[Original composition of global inputs]}


\section{Which arithmetic data survive a change of ordinate?}
\label{sec:endpoint-fields}

Suppose that a prime pair has already been chosen and that a calculation needs the same cohomological bound at two scales. There is a simple way to obtain such a bound inside a window that the original proof has already selected. Evaluate its universal quantifier twice. The interesting question arises when a proposed analytic repair asks for an endpoint outside that window. At this endpoint we know the primes and the page function. We may even know the cardinality bound on the exceptional sets. Which of these facts make the local argument available there? We answer this question by rebuilding the field estimates with an explicit hypothesis on the weights used at the new ordinate. The reconstruction has a second consequence. Over intervals of the width used in the analytic theorem, two successful tests of these weights already certify every intermediate ordinate.

Throughout this section $X$ is the outer scale, $x$ an ordinate in its range, $q,d$ the same prime pair at every ordinate, and $C_t$ the exponent of the summation cutoff. Write $e(x)$ for the page function and $k$ for an integer weight. These two integers have different jobs. The field at the endpoint depends on $e(x)$ and can change when $x$ changes. The exceptional integers are possible values of $k$. For fixed $X,q,d$ and one choice of the arithmetic witnesses, the same sets of integers are tested at every $x$. In particular, a shared pair and shared exceptional sets do not require a shared value of $e(x)$, or a shared concrete field realizing the character at the two endpoints.

\subsection{The smallest local premise used by the field calculation}

Let $E$ be one finite exceptional set. For a real exponent $\eta$ define
\[
   A_{\eta}(E;t,x)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
     \forall k\in E,\qquad 1\leq k<t(x)\ \Longrightarrow\ k\leq x^{\eta}.
\]
The order of the inequalities is important. The source sum only demands individual bounds at the positive weights $k<t(x)$. Its more convenient original window predicate covers every exceptional $k\leq x^{C_t}$. Under the page cutoff, $t(x)=\lfloor x^{C_t}\rfloor$. When $x^{C_t}$ is an integer $k$, the original predicate sees $k$ immediately. The summation first sees $k$ when $x^{C_t}$ reaches $k+1$. The original predicate remains a sufficient way to feed the argument. It is useful to record what information is lost when one uses it in reverse to diagnose a failed estimate.

The relaxed premise appears in the proof at one specific branch. Fix a prime $\ell$, base field, finite rank bound $R$, and the original exceptional set supplied for the cutoff $Z=\lfloor X^{C_t}\rfloor$. For $k\notin E$, the first field bound supplies finiteness and controls the integer length by a term involving $R$, the layer index, a field constant, and $\log_{\ell} Z$. For $k\in E$, the alternative estimate on a reference layer controls the length through a factor of the shape
\[
    \exp\!\left((2kn)^{C_K}\log(2|D|)\right),
\]
where $n$ is the field degree and $D$ its discriminant. Only this second branch uses a smallness hypothesis on $k$. The inequality is applied to the particular $k$ for which the calculation has entered that branch. Requiring simultaneous smallness of all the active exceptional integers is a way of discharging each such query in the sum. An exceptional integer outside the sum prompts no query.

Suppose $n\leq n_0$, $k\leq x^{w\epsilon/C_K}$, $C_K>0$, and $\log(2|D|)\leq c_0 x^{r\epsilon}$, with $c_0\geq0$ and $x\geq1$. Raising the weight inequality to $C_K$ and multiplying the two estimates gives
\[
 (2kn)^{C_K}\log(2|D|)
 \leq (2n_0)^{C_K}c_0\,x^{(w+r)\epsilon}.
\]
Writing the product explicitly shows which exponent could pay for a larger exceptional weight. The moving even reference field depends on $q,d$. Its logarithmic discriminant estimate, derived using the outer pair-size condition, contributes $r=1/4$. The odd reference layer is fixed before $X$ and the pair, so its logarithmic discriminant can be used as the constant $c_0$, with $r=0$. The difference survives all the way through the field-valued conclusion. It would be lost if all the primes were first pooled into a single set and tested at the worst exponent.

The remaining field lengths admit a common upper envelope
\[
      C(1+\log x)+2\exp\!\left(Cx^{(w+r)\epsilon}\right).
\]
The $x^{C_t}$ possible summands multiply this envelope. Taking the logarithm of the target $\exp(x^{4\epsilon/5})$ reveals the needed comparison. The additive $C_t\log x$, all fixed constants, and the main $Cx^{(w+r)\epsilon}$ are eventually absorbed if $w+r<4/5$. The resulting sufficient allowances are
\[
   w_A<\frac{11}{20}\quad\text{for the moving even field},
   \qquad
   w_B<\frac45\quad\text{for the fixed odd fields}.
\]
For an enlargement relative to the original $w=1/2$, choose both weights at least $1/2$ and strictly inside these ranges. One can take different values in the two roles. A larger $w$ admits more exceptional integers at a fixed $x>1$. The proof allows $w$ to approach its ceiling only after a new threshold has been selected. It supplies no common starting $X$ for a sequence of weights approaching the boundary. At equality a leading coefficient $C>1$ may already defeat the proposed absorption, so this particular bound cannot certify that boundary with an arbitrary constant. None of this measures the true growth of the cohomological length. Sharper field estimates could change the available margins.

\subsection{Keeping the quantifiers in their source order}

A common objection to doing a pointwise calculation is that the field constants might quietly change at the second endpoint. The source prevents some changes and permits others. The common-family construction makes these distinctions testable. From the three arithmetic inputs called \texttt{LemmaK}, \texttt{FixedIn}, and \texttt{TwoIn}, it chooses a package $K$ of two rank functions and a positive exponent $C_K$. As in the source, $C_K$ is raised to at least one using monotonicity. The even rank is chosen from the uniform moving-field input. For the odd ranks the construction chooses a witness when an appropriate field exists. Where the field hypotheses cannot be met, the universal proposition about such fields holds vacuously. The proof does not turn these choices into a numerical table of ranks.

Next fix the base character or field, the finite list of odd primes, $C_t$, the two weights, and $\epsilon$. The range exponent $J$ retains the expression from the source. An outer threshold handles the moving term and the finitely many odd thresholds simultaneously. For example, the maximum of the moving threshold, one, and a sum of absolute values of the odd thresholds bounds all of them. The conclusion is uniform in $X$ beyond that threshold. Its usable ordinates satisfy $X^{1/J}\leq x\leq X$. This interval is where the argument can convert an outer bound on the pair discriminant into the required local one, and can charge $\log Z$ to $O(1+\log x)$. An $x$ outside this range cannot be inserted on the strength of its integer-weight test alone.

Choose admitted page and cutoff functions, an outer $X$, and an admissible $q,d$ for that $X$. The even field and its exceptional set $E_A$ can now be chosen once. For each odd prime $\ell$, choose a set $E_{B,\ell}$ once at the common cutoff $Z$; the odd construction itself does not need $q,d$. The theorem retains
\[
    |E_A|\leq R_0(M),\qquad
    |E_{B,\ell}|\leq R_\ell(L_\ell).
\]
Only after the sets are fixed is $x$ introduced. If its even active test and every odd active test hold, the outputs are the original predicates \texttt{BaAt} and \texttt{BbAt}. The former quantifies over fields with the requested composite-field structure at $e(x),q,d$. The latter quantifies over each retained odd prime, a realization of its abelian base, and a layer of the specified depth. The proof transfers the odd estimate along the prescribed field isomorphism. These universal field clauses matter, since height and vanishing must be able to request the particular layers belonging to its host.

There is no assumption here that either list of centers has been evaluated. Having $|E_A|\leq R_0$ suffices for the original interval-selection theorem, which discards intervals where weights could be troublesome. It gives little information about a prescribed ordinate. The singleton consisting of the first heavy, active integer can defeat that ordinate when the allowance is positive. Conversely, a displayed finite set of integers is useful for testing this theorem only after its relationship to the field witness has been established. Our finite controls exercise the logic of the test, without assigning their model sets to a character.

\subsection{From the two field estimates to \texorpdfstring{$V$}{V} at an ordinate}

The local height argument starts from the original \texttt{PointHV} proposition. It supplies a constant and two auxiliary primes once the character and the target odd layer indices have been specified. These data precede $x$. For a fixed pair it suffices that each prime be greater than $2X^{C_t}+1$, and that both exceed a finite list determined by the character and the two auxiliary primes. At the chosen $x\leq X$, admissibility bounds $t(x)$ by $X^{C_t}$, which gives the size requirement for the host at $x$. The odd estimate \texttt{BbAt} then supplies the requested layer bound for every odd exception prime of the character. Applying the pointwise height theorem and identifying the host conductor gives the original \texttt{HVAt} for that $x$. In the published window proof a clean interval delivers \texttt{BbAt} at each ordinate. Once the datum at a single ordinate is in hand, the height calculation uses this local value.

For the final field step, the local even predicate controls the two-primary piece, the height predicate controls the plus piece, and the original \texttt{ThmBc} controls the minus piece. The last input holds for arbitrary admissible scales once the pair and field hypotheses are satisfied. For a character field $F$ at $e(x),q,d$, the source constructs its CM structure, passes to the composite-field statement, and uses the page bound to control $\log(\mathrm{cond}(F)t(x))$. The inequality $\log(qd)\leq x^{\epsilon/4}$ is needed here as well. It follows at every permitted $x$ from the same outer hypothesis $\log(qd)\leq X^{\epsilon/(4J)}$. Fixed constants and both remaining error terms are absorbed beyond a common lower threshold. The conclusion is the source's \texttt{VAt}, with its universal quantifier over character fields and with $e(x)$ evaluated at the requested ordinate.

To use this inference twice, choose $x_-$ and $x_+$ in the same outer range above all thresholds. Keep the input \texttt{WinPair} witness and its scale parameter fixed alongside the pair, the page function, and the exceptional sets. Verify the two lists of weight tests at each endpoint. Evaluating the final universal statement twice produces \texttt{VAt} at both. This distinguishes two questions that a phrase such as ``the same witness'' can conceal. The conditional implication just proved needs no new selection between its two applications. Finding an $X,q,d$ and two ordinates satisfying every premise at once, with the placements required by a particular analytic experiment, remains a separate existence question. The universal conclusion also allows the character field requested at the first endpoint to differ from the one requested at the second.

\subsection{A failure at one integer, and a restriction on hidden holes}

For positive $\eta,C_t$ and one positive exceptional $k$, the exact forbidden interval for the active test is
\[
   \left[(k+1)^{1/C_t},\ k^{1/\eta}\right).
\]
An interval with its left end at or above its right end is empty. Indeed, activity means $k<\lfloor x^{C_t}\rfloor$, equivalently $k+1\leq x^{C_t}$, and excess weight means $x^\eta<k$. In the original test the left boundary is $k^{1/C_t}$. Thus the literal left endpoint of the original band is safe from its own integer, and the right endpoint is safe by equality. For a relaxed exponent larger than the original one, the right boundary moves inward as well. This observation certifies individual labels. If other labels can interfere, one must take the union of their forbidden intervals. At an interior endpoint of an overlapping family a neighbor can still forbid the ordinate.

There is a stronger statement when both endpoints are tested with the same frozen set. Let $a\leq x\leq b$, let the allowed weight be nondecreasing, and let $t$ be nondecreasing. Assume
\[
       A_\eta(E;t,a),\qquad A_\eta(E;t,b),\qquad
       b^\eta<t(a).
\]
Then $A_\eta(E;t,x)$ holds at every intermediate $x$. For a proof take any positive $k\in E$ active at $x$. If $k<t(a)$, the left certificate gives $k\leq a^\eta\leq x^\eta$. Otherwise $k\geq t(a)$, and it is active at $b$ because $t(x)\leq t(b)$. The right certificate gives $k\leq b^\eta<t(a)$, a contradiction. This argument does not count the exceptional integers. The two endpoints divide them according to the earlier summation cutoff, and the strict crossover prevents a weight from straddling the division. The right endpoint alone cannot make the same inference since its weight allowance is larger.

For $t(x)=\lfloor x^{C_t}\rfloor$, $C_t\geq2$, $a\geq2$, $b\leq a^\lambda$, and $0\leq\lambda\eta\leq1$ with $\eta\geq0$, one has
\[
        b^\eta\leq a<\lfloor a^{C_t}\rfloor .
\]
The strict inequality follows from $a^2\geq a+2$ and $a^{C_t}\geq a^2$. This elementary version suffices without computing any asymptotic threshold. For the source choice $C_t=401$, $C_K\geq1$ and $0<\epsilon<1/52$, each relaxed even and odd exponent in the enlargement regime is less than $1/65$. Every analytic width $\lambda=3/2$ therefore satisfies the hypothesis. In fact the same convenient estimate reaches $\lambda=65$. This is a conservative sufficient range. The more general comparison required is just $\lfloor a^{C_t}\rfloor>b^\eta$. For $b=a^\lambda$ it eventually holds whenever $C_t>\lambda\eta$.

This observation changes how to design an endpoint experiment. If an interval of analytic width has two endpoints certified solely by these arithmetic weights for one fixed outer witness, there can be no remaining hole in the active-weight tests between them. Reapply the field proof there, provided the outer range and local floor threshold cover the interval. Endpoint interpolation could still matter if an endpoint is obtained by a genuinely different certificate, if only prime-sum bounds are known at the endpoints, or if some other premise changes through the interval. Two isolated \texttt{VAt} facts do not reveal which sufficient tests produced them. Inferring the active-weight predicates from those conclusions would reverse a one-way implication.

\subsection{Exact controls and the scope of the replay}

To check the interval boundary rather than merely illustrate it, we evaluated all 256 subsets of the eight positive integers from one through eight. With $C_t=2$, $\eta=1$, the mesh consisted of the 29 rational points from one through eight separated by one quarter. We tested every ordered choice of distinct increasing endpoints. Endpoints and interior points were evaluated using exact rational arithmetic and integer floors. Among 103,936 family--interval combinations, 13,576 passed both endpoints. Exactly 6,392 of these satisfied the strict crossover, and all 6,392 passed at every intervening mesh point. The continuum conclusion comes from the preceding proof. The counts test the implementation and expose endpoint conventions that a floating-point power could blur.

At equality between the earlier cutoff and the later allowance, 82 endpoint-passing combinations contained an obstructed interior point. There were another 5,374 such combinations below the strict crossover. The smallest convenient example for an equality failure is $E=\{4\}$, $a=2$, $b=4$. At $2$ the cutoff is four and the weight four is inactive. At $4$ the weight is allowed by equality. At $5/2$ it is active and exceeds the allowance. Removing the strict inequality admits this obstruction. An overlap control uses $E=\{9,16\}$. The outer endpoints $3$ and $16$ pass. At $4$, the integer $16$ is still inactive but $9$ obstructs. At $9$, the integer $9$ has become small while $16$ obstructs. Thus even both nominal boundary equalities are uninformative about other overlapping integers. These counterexamples test logical premises; the values of $\epsilon,C_t$ used in the finite exercise are chosen to make the arithmetic inspectable.

The supplement imports the original \texttt{ThmBabOf}, \texttt{ThmHV}, \texttt{AdmissibleOfPageN} and \texttt{PairGood} modules at Git revision \texttt{40e56bab}. The source-to-source adaptations retain the actual number-field length, layer, discriminant, and character-field predicates. This matters because a large class of scalar inequalities can have the desired exponents and nevertheless fail to transport through a change of field or an existentially chosen constant. The ledger records the Git blob for each of the 701 original project modules in the import closure, the pinned Mathlib revision, Lean version, hashes for reusable objects, and the kernel report for our supplement. Reusing a previously built module and independently replaying the new endpoint object are different operations. Section-level verification refers to the declarations reported by the accompanying replay. The original proof's headline endpoint and the full upstream library were outside this build.

The most informative remaining arithmetic test would evaluate one actual frozen exceptional integer, or obtain an effective enclosure on its location, for the same cutoff and pair appearing in the analytic question. This would convert a conditional coverage statement into an observation about an identified field. The present result narrows the target. For an analytic-width interval, a successful evaluation at just two endpoints would certify the complete range of active-weight tests. If those endpoints were established through a different method, the first question to ask would be which arithmetic predicate that method actually returns. That distinction decides whether the argument extends through the interval.

\paragraph{Reading the source across the interface.}
One can check the inputs in a short, directed reading of the original archive. The definitions of the package $K$, the range $J$, and the even and odd endpoint obligations sit together in \texttt{PrimeSetBoundDataOfPrimitiveChar}. That file also supplies the odd-prime and root-of-unity premise for the specific index set attached to a primitive character. Thus a concrete character introduces no separate choice of its auxiliary odd prime list in the endpoint theorem. The field arguments at issue here begin after the character and this list are given. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/AutomorphicVarieties/PrimeSetBoundDataOfPrimitiveChar.lean}{Pinned definitions and primitive-character specialization.}

The upstream proof of the common-family input passes from a uniform tower estimate to the fixed and moving cases. Its wrapper invokes the literature input for Ferrero--Washington together with a tower bound constructed using the Poitou--Tate, local-finiteness and local-duality inputs. On the height side a different wrapper supplies the pointwise height proposition by adding the two groups of counting estimates. Their overlap in prerequisites explains why an independently selected prime witness for each point would make a poor experiment. Such separate witnesses might satisfy all their own local conclusions while giving no reusable statement about one character, one pair, and one pointwise family. The local theorem retains those shared variables explicitly; its conditional arithmetic inputs can be read directly from its signature. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/AutomorphicVarieties/LayeredIsomorphisms/ThmBabOfLemmaK.lean}{Original moving and fixed wrappers.} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/AutomorphicVarieties/HeightAndVanishing/JointWinOfCounts.lean}{Original counting and height wrappers.}

There is a related distinction concerning witness identity across experiments. The published window theorem exports the existence of a window and field estimates throughout it. It hides the exceptional sets used by its proof. Even if our construction follows exactly the same choice operations in the pinned source, an already-exported existential window does not expose the identity of the finite sets selected behind it. The uniformity established here holds inside one invocation of the strengthened construction. To attach its actual $E_A,E_{B,\ell}$ to a previously materialized prime row or to a host chosen in another proof, one must keep those data in scope from the start or exhibit an explicit compatibility argument. Matching the cardinality bounds or the names of the input hypotheses does not identify the witnesses. This is also the reason the finite exact controls are reported with their explicit sets: the same two scales can answer a different mathematical question when two invocations have silently chosen different sets.

\paragraph{One late prime pair and one collection of exceptional sets.}
The final declaration in the supplement closes the prime-selection part of the quantifier chain. Its input \texttt{PairEx} supplies a full \texttt{PairGood} at every sufficiently large base scale $V$, with twist estimates on $[V,V^J]$ and the source zero-box conditions. Since $J\geq1$ for the normalized $K$, take $V=X^{1/J}$ and use $V^J=X$. Increasing the common outer threshold makes both primes exceed $2X^{C_t}+1$ and every member of the finite host list, and gives $\log(qd)\leq X^{\epsilon/(4J)}$. The pair is therefore available before the exceptional sets and every subsequent endpoint test. Consequently, for every sufficiently large $X$, there exist $q,d,E_A,(E_{B,\ell})$ such that the full pair bound holds on the outer range and every ordinate passing the stated tests has the original \texttt{VAt} there. From the arithmetic inputs this provides the pair uniformly in the endpoints. The remaining placement question concerns the frozen sets belonging to that actual pair. The file \texttt{kernel.json} records independent kernel replay of the supplement and the axiom reports for fifteen declarations, including the prime-selection and field-filling statements. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/PairGood.lean}{Original full prime-pair predicate.}

A further issue arises when moving from the arithmetic ordinate to the outer analytic variable. In the source a fixed factor $k=c^{1/(2+8\epsilon)}\geq1$ can rescale the page ordinate by $x=kx'$. An analytic interval $[a,b]$ with $b\leq a^{3/2}$ therefore maps to $u=a/k$, $v=b/k$ satisfying $v\leq k^{1/2}u^{3/2}$. At $u\geq k$, this implies $v\leq u^2$. The strict-crossover argument applies because $2\eta<1$ in the source regime. This specifies which threshold can absorb the rescaling and explains why a close comparison of the two variables is necessary. A proof of the unscaled $3/2$ case by itself would leave the factor $k^{1/2}$ unaccounted for. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/WinPairs.lean}{Pinned rescaling in the prime-pair construction.}


\section{A sharp gap bound from the freedom to choose the outer scale}
\label{sec:outer-endpoint-gap}
The preceding arguments concern estimates available at the scales selected inside a particular arithmetic block. There is one further way to place endpoints. The hypotheses used to select the inner block are available for \emph{every} sufficiently large outer scale. Different outer scales may produce different witnesses. If each witness ultimately marks ordinates of the same fixed scalar function, we can take their union. The distinction between an arbitrarily late outer scale and every sufficiently late outer scale then has an exact geometric consequence.

\subsection{The information kept from the construction}
Write \(U=\log X\) for the outer scale, \(w=\log W\) for the inner start, \(c=\log k\ge0\) for a fixed rescaling, and \(v=\log(x/k)\) for the target coordinate. In this section a mark means membership in one fixed set \(S\) of \(v\)-coordinates. To conclude a prime-sum estimate from a mark, the proof must already have identified this set with ordinates admitting the relevant common-family scalar comparison. The following real-variable argument makes no use of any other facts about the arithmetic. Suppose there exist fixed \(J>0,c\ge0,U_0\) such that
\begin{equation}
 \forall U\ge U_0\quad\exists w\in[U/J,U/2]\quad
       [w,\min(2w,U-c)]\subseteq S .
 \label{eq:outer-universal}
\end{equation}
We may increase \(U_0\) so that \(U_0>0\) and \(U_0\ge2c\), ensuring that these intervals are nonempty. Existence of \(w\) requires \(J\ge2\). The lower and upper bounds on \(w\) correspond to \(X^{1/J}\le W\) and \(W^2\le X\). The two caps on \(v\) keep \(x/k\le W^2\) and \(x\le X\), respectively. In the original file the inner start and prime pair are selected after the outer scale is fixed. The wider marked segment in \eqref{eq:outer-universal} uses the more local reading of the proof addressed in this study; the source's exported selector itself states a selected \(3/2\)-power window. A deduction using that exported statement alone must not silently upgrade it to \eqref{eq:outer-universal}. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/WinPairs.lean#L23-L78}{[Original outer-scale and local-witness construction]}

If \(2\le J\le4\), every sufficiently late \(v\) is marked. To prove this, choose \(U=2v\), with \(v\ge c\) and \(2v\ge U_0\). The available witness has \(w\le v\) and \(2w\ge4v/J\ge v\). In addition \(U-c=2v-c\ge v\), so the clipped segment contains \(v\). All inequalities allow equality. No continuity or uniform choice of \(w\) as a function of \(U\) was used. Nor does this case analysis establish \(J\le4\) for an actual constructed character family. Constants that establish arithmetic supply can be very large. The estimate gives meaning to the size of any such constant once available.

For \(J>4\) put \(q=J/4>1\). Every sufficiently late \emph{closed} interval \([L,qL]\) intersects \(S\). Indeed choose \(U=JL/2\). Then \(L/2\le w\le qL\), and both \(2w\) and \(U-c\) are at least \(L\) once \(L\) is sufficiently large. Thus the two closed intervals intersect. More generally, suppose \((\ell,r)\) is mark-free and \(\ell\) is sufficiently late. Necessarily
\[
                            r/\ell \le q .
\]
If \(r>q\ell\), choose \(U\) strictly between \(J\ell/2\) and \(2r\). It supplies \(w<r\) and \(\min(2w,U-c)>\ell\); because the segment is nonempty, it intersects the open interval. Equivalently a slightly smaller closed hitting window would fit strictly inside the purported empty interval. The threshold on \(\ell\) can be chosen once for the fixed \(J,c,U_0\). It does not depend on the prime pair selected in a later outer block.

These statements also give quantitative bracketing without any topological closure assumption on \(S\). A sufficiently late target \(v\) has a marked ordinate in \([v/q,v]\) and another in \([v,qv]\), by applying the closed hitting theorem at starts \(v/q\) and \(v\). If \(v\) itself is unmarked, the two marks are strictly on opposite sides. If only one nearby mark is needed, the interval \([v/\sqrt q,\sqrt q\,v]\) suffices. These ratios refer to the variable \(v=\log(x/k)\). For example, a mark-free original-scale interval with ends \(a,b\) has \(\log(b/k)\le q\log(a/k)\), or \(b\le k(a/k)^q\), at late scales. This permits a logarithmic gap proportional to \(\log(a/k)\); it does not prove a small missing logarithmic length of the kind that bounds interpolation error.

It helps to state what a two-endpoint application would require. Start with an uncovered ordinate and apply the two closed hits. Record both endpoints on the original scale, their individual ceilings, and the curvature information on the whole interval between them. The scalar interpolation problem can now be posed without ever asking the two prime pairs to agree. The geometry has supplied the existence of the input endpoints. It has supplied neither their numerical values nor an estimate small enough for a chosen error budget. For instance, even when the ratio of the endpoint ordinates in the logarithmic variable is two, doubling that variable squares the rescaled original argument. A factor of two in this coordinate consequently becomes progressively less restrictive for local interpolation as the target moves out. A theorem bounding an average or a derivative-weighted integral would have to exploit its weighting, the growth of curvature, or additional information on how the admissible blocks overlap. One can test stronger constraints on such overlaps by asking whether the single switching construction below could satisfy them. Any constraint already satisfied by that construction leaves the sharp geometric obstruction in place. If the constraint rules it out, a quantitative replacement for the gap bound still requires its own argument.

\subsection{One selection rule attains infinitely many limiting gaps}
Sharpness should be tested against the full \(\forall U\) premise. Choosing a different collection of bad examples for every proposed endpoint would not supply one consistent set at all outer scales. Fix \(J>4\). Select a large first \(\ell_1>0\), put \(r_i=q\ell_i\), and choose
\(\ell_{i+1}>Jr_i\) recursively. Let \(T_i=J\ell_i/2=2r_i\). At every sufficiently large \(U\) use this single rule:
\[
 w(U)=
 \begin{cases}
 U/2,& T_i<U<Jr_i\quad\text{for some }i,\\
 U/J,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
Declare exactly the union of its supplied closed segments to be marked. The switching intervals are disjoint; each value of \(w\) satisfies the required outer inequalities. Choose \(\ell_1\) large enough that \(T_i-c\ge\ell_i\) for each \(i\) and that all switch points lie beyond \(U_0\).

On a low branch a segment could meet \((\ell_i,r_i)\) only if \(2U/J>\ell_i\) and \(U/J<r_i\). These conditions place \(U\) strictly inside the \(i\)-th switching interval, where the branch is high. On a high branch serving the \(i\)-th interval the starting ordinate \(U/2\) already exceeds \(r_i\). It also exceeds each previous \(r_j\); the segment ends no later than \(U<Jr_i<\ell_{i+1}\), so it misses every later gap. This proves avoidance simultaneously for the infinitely many holes and every large outer scale. At \(U=T_i\) the low branch ends precisely at \(\ell_i\), including the clipped endpoint. At \(U=Jr_i\) the low branch starts at \(r_i\). Both endpoints are literally marked by single witnesses from different outer scales. Every gap has ratio exactly \(q\), with no marked point in its interior. At \(v=\sqrt{\ell_i r_i}\) this also attains the worst possible factor \(\sqrt q\) for a nearest mark. Hence neither closing the inequalities in the hitting result nor distinguishing two endpoint witnesses makes the sharp example disappear.

For example take \(J=8\), \(c=7\), \(\ell_1=16\) and \(r_1=32\). The low choice at \(U=64\) has \(w=8\) and supplies \([8,16]\). For \(64<U<256\) the high segment starts above 32. At \(U=256\) the low choice starts exactly at 32. All earlier low segments end no later than 16 and all immediately subsequent low segments start at or beyond 32. The affine shifts are applied to the endpoints before converting back to the ordinate \(x\). Although its endpoints are marked, the size of the gap in the \(\log x\) integration measure is \(32-16=16\). The same construction at late \(\ell_i\) makes that integration width grow without bound. Counting endpoints alone is therefore too weak to price the area on all the gaps. This construction concerns the information in \eqref{eq:outer-universal}; it imposes none of the number-field, rank or cohomological restrictions on the actual selection.

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\bandfigure{.98\linewidth}{supply}\caption{An admissible schedule can place a marked interval on either side of the same gap. At \(U=64\) one interval reaches 16; at \(U=256\) another starts at 32. Ticks within each row use the same logarithmic coordinate. The rightmost plotted segments are clipped to the display.}\end{figure}

\subsection{Adversarial controls and limits of the deduction}
Removing universality in the outer scale changes the answer even at \(J=2\). Let supply occur only at \(U_1=4,\ U_{i+1}=U_i^2\), take \(c=0\), and mark \([U_i/2,U_i]\) for each \(i\). The construction is cofinal and each witness meets all the range requirements at its designated outer scale. Between consecutive marked segments the gap ratio is \(U_i/2\), tending to infinity. This control identifies the logical work done by the universal quantifier on large \(U\). Another possible weakening would allow a different target family for every block. In that setting the union still has geometric coverage, but its two endpoint estimates need not constrain a single prime sum. Similarly, requiring an identical prime pair for both endpoints would add a premise to a scalar conclusion that may legitimately have different local witnesses.

The supplemental rational checks exercised 1,020 cases at and below the \(J=4\) transition, 1,020 closed threshold hits above it, and 3,060 strict interior hits. Five model parameters, including \(J=401/100\) just beyond the transition and \(J=200\), were used to check switch boundaries and five successive sharp gaps each. The schedule test splits the outer axis at the switches, clip changes and possible band crossings, testing the resulting boundary points and open cells on a finite interval. These finite checks expose reversed inequalities, forgotten clipping and one-sided endpoint mistakes. The result for every real outer scale and infinitely many gaps follows from the preceding argument. The calculation gives no numerical value of \(J\) or defect location for a particular arithmetic family and does not identify an actual arithmetic gap. The supplement \texttt{GapGeometry} checks the saturation and closed-hitting implications against the explicit supply predicate with \(q=J/4\). The infinite extremizing schedule and its sharpness remain written arguments. Its positive conclusion is the simultaneous availability of coarse bracketing marks under the stated uniform supply. The issue of whether their spacing or additional arithmetic compatibility suffices for a stronger bound remains distinct.


\subsection{How demanding is the saturation regime in the original construction?}
