One can locate an actual numerical scale in the original algorithm without guessing the unknown exception sets. The field-window constructor chooses
\[
 J_{\rm field}=2^{R+2}\left(\frac{2C_K C_t}{\varepsilon}\right)^R
              =4\left(\frac{4 C_K C_t}{\varepsilon}\right)^R ,
 \qquad R=R_0(M)+\sum_{\ell\in B'}R_\ell(L_\ell).
\]
The terms in \(R\) are rank bounds supplied by prior existence statements. A rank bound can exceed the number of exceptional integers at a particular outer cutoff. If this particular \(R\) vanishes, the displayed field search has \(J_{\rm field}=4\). If \(R\ge1\), \(C_K\ge1\), \(C_t=401\), and \(0<\varepsilon<1/52\), already \(J_{\rm field}>333632\). The elementary arithmetic at \(R=1\) is \(8\cdot 2\cdot401\cdot52=333632\); each further unit of \(R\) multiplies the estimate by more than \(83408\). The subsequent admissibility constructor explicitly allows enlargement of its parameter. Thus the saturation theorem at four is mathematically exact for the supply interface, while very demanding for this particular route to it. Even empty exception sets at a finite outer cutoff do not justify replacing the uniform bound \(R\) by zero. Conversely, a very large allowed search range is only a weak guarantee on the location of a window. It is no observation that the selector actually produces enormous gaps. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/AutomorphicVarieties/LayeredIsomorphisms/ThmBabOf.lean#L15-L29}{[Construction of the field search constant]}\quad\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/AutomorphicVarieties/CMFieldExponentialBounds/AdmissibleOfPageN.lean#L45-L60}{[Use of the upper search parameter]}

The failure to reach a square when \(k>1\) is small in exponent and large in distance. At the adverse endpoint \(X=W^2\), a square window based at \(kW\) asks for \(k^2W^2\), while the shared source range ends at \(W^2\). The missing distance is \((k^2-1)W^2\); its logarithmic length is the constant \(2\log k\). For every fixed \(\lambda<2\) the power margin eventually absorbs that constant in logarithmic coordinates. At \(\lambda=2\) the margin vanishes. One might move the start to \(W\) and use the square window ending at \(W^2\), but the left endpoint would then precede the first secured point \(kW\) by \((k-1)W\). A one-ended Lipschitz estimate of order \(W^\theta\) for \(\theta<1\) cannot guarantee either repair when \(k>1\) is fixed. This explains why an arithmetic reason for removing the rescaling is qualitatively stronger than simply sampling more finely near a boundary.


\section{How far a scalar estimate travels beyond its witness}
\label{sec:slope}
The preceding questions concern membership in an arithmetic domain. The analytic criterion asks for an upper bound on a real function. These are distinct pieces of information even when the first implies the second. Suppose the last certified point of a window is \(t\). Which nearby evaluations of the \emph{very same sum} can we certify without doing arithmetic at the nearby point? Continuity alone supplies a radius depending on the value and on \(t\). To compare that radius with a growing hole we need a modulus uniform over all sufficiently large \(t\).

\subsection{A uniform bound from periodic cancellation}
Fix a finite admissible positive character family \(F\) in the sense of the original source. A principal character appearing in it has modulus one. At real \(y\ge 1\) its sum is a finite real linear combination of the real parts of
\[
 H_\chi(y)=\sum_{n\le y}\Lambda(n)\chi(n)(y/n-1)+
 \begin{cases}
  -y\log y+(1+\gamma)y,&\chi=1,\\[2pt]
  \alpha_\chi y,&\chi\ne1,\qquad
   \alpha_\chi=L'(1,\chi)/L(1,\chi).
 \end{cases}
\]
At integer knots the incoming ramp vanishes, so the function is continuous. Every compact interval intersects only finitely many pieces. We prove the existence of a finite \(K_F>0\) such that \(|G_F(y)-G_F(t)|\le K_F|y-t|\) for every \(t,y\ge1\). The constant depends on the actual finite family, including its weights. Our argument gives no uniform numerical constant over the succession of possible families or levels. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/CharFamily.lean#L10-L36}{[Original ramp, correction and admissibility]}

For a nonprincipal character its partial sums are bounded because it is periodic and has sum zero over one period. Write
\[
 L=\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{\chi(m)}m,\qquad B=\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{\chi(m)\log m}{m},
 \qquad T(u)=\sum_{m>u}\frac{\chi(m)}m .
\]
Partial summation gives \(|T(u)|\ll_\chi 1/u\) for real \(u\ge1\), and ensures convergence of \(B\). More precisely, partial summation against \(x^{-s}\) on compact subsets of \(\Re(s)>0\) produces the integral of a bounded partial-sum function times \(s x^{-s-1}\). This integral and its derivative converge locally uniformly. They agree with the usual Dirichlet series where \(\Re(s)>1\) and therefore continue it to a neighborhood of 1. This identifies \(L=L(1,\chi)\) and \(B=-L'(1,\chi)\). Nonvanishing at 1 for nonprincipal characters permits division by \(L\). There is no division by a value of the function at a moving test height. \href{https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/Nonvanishing.lean}{[Mathlib, nonvanishing at one]}

For an integer \(N\ge1\) let \(A_N=\sum_{d\le N}\Lambda(d)\chi(d)/d\) and \(B_N=\sum_{n\le N}\chi(n)\log(n)/n\). Using \(\log n=\sum_{d\mid n}\Lambda(d)\) and complete multiplicativity gives a finite identity in complex numbers,
\[
 B_N=\sum_{d\le N}\frac{\Lambda(d)\chi(d)}d\sum_{m\le N/d}\frac{\chi(m)}m .
\]
Consequently the right derivative of \(H_\chi\) on \([N,N+1)\) obeys
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slope}
 L(A_N+\alpha_\chi)= B_N-B+
       \sum_{d\le N}\frac{\Lambda(d)\chi(d)}d T(N/d).
\end{equation}
The second term in absolute value is at most a character-dependent constant times \(\psi(N)/N\), where \(\psi(N)=\sum_{d\le N}\Lambda(d)\). The elementary Chebyshev estimate \(\psi(N)\ll N\) bounds this uniformly. The first term is bounded by convergence. This proof gives control between every pair of adjacent knots, including prime powers. Simply taking absolute values in the original derivative would have lost the cancellation in the inner character sum. \href{https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/NumberTheory/Chebyshev.lean}{[Mathlib, Chebyshev estimates]}

For modulus one,
\(\log(N!)=\sum_{d\le N}\Lambda(d)\lfloor N/d\rfloor\).
Replacing each floor by its argument gives absolute error at most \(\psi(N)\). Integrating \(\log x\) or comparing consecutive rectangles gives \(\log(N!)/N=\log N+O(1)\). Thus \(\sum_{d\le N}\Lambda(d)/d=\log N+O(1)\), uniformly including the finite initial range after increasing the constant. On \([N,N+1)\) the derivative differs from this by \(-\log y+\gamma\), and \(0\le\log y-\log N\le\log 2\). Summing the piecewise derivative bounds over any compact interval gives the claim for modulus one. Taking the finite weighted sum of the individual bounds, then increasing it by one if needed, supplies \(K_F>0\). This proof is a written deduction. The Lean replay in this part starts from the Lipschitz inequality as an explicit input.

\subsection{A uniform number for one genuine arithmetic function}
For the real primitive character modulo four we tested the candidate \(K=2\). A finite maximum observed on the first thousand cells cannot justify a global bound. To cover the tail, use the alternating odd terms of \(L=1-1/3+1/5-\cdots\) and \(B\). For \(N\ge3\),
\[
 |T(u)|\le 1/u\quad(u\ge1),\qquad |B-B_N|\le(\log N)/N.
\]
The next nonzero odd denominator strictly exceeds the real cutoff. Also \((\log x)/x\) decreases for \(x\ge3\). The eight-term even partial sum gives \(L\ge33976/45045>3/4\). There is an elementary explicit upper bound
\[
 \psi(N)\le 2N\log2+(1+\log_2 N)\log N .
\]
Indeed, put \(m=\lfloor N/2\rfloor\). For each prime power \(p^j\in(m,N]\) the valuation of \(\binom{N}{m}\) receives a unit contribution from that power except possibly at \(p^j=m+1\) when \(N\) is odd. This follows directly by subtracting the three factorial valuations term by term. One extra factor \(p\) at that middle prime power suffices. Hence \(\psi(N)-\psi(m)\le N\log 2+\log N\). Iterating the halving bound costs at most \(2N\log2\) in the first term and at most \((1+\log_2 N)\log N\) in the second. The parity exception is observable already at \(N=3\). In 117 of the 1,023 integer tests through 1,024, divisibility without the correction failed. Every corrected divisibility test passed.

Inserting these estimates into \eqref{eq:slope} gives
\[
 |A_N+\alpha_{\chi_4}|\le
 \frac{2\log2+(2+\log_2 N)(\log N)/N}{33976/45045}.
\]
For \(N\ge1024\) both \((\log N)/N\) and \((\log N)^2/N\) decrease. Directed evaluation at 1,024 gives an upper bound \(1.9456249440772\). On the complementary 1,023 cells, outward evaluation encloses all right slopes in \([-0.17261654038041,0.24560958477732]\). These two arguments cover adjacent ranges and yield the advertised global constant. The large disparity between the two bounds is an accounting fact about the uniform tail estimate. The computation gives no evidence that an actual tail slope nearly reaches two.

As a separate exact check, the finite divisor identity was expanded in rational coefficients of each \(\log p\) for \(N=16,64,257,511,1024,4096\). At the final size 564 prime coefficients were compared. Omitting the prime square \(9\) changes the coefficient of \(\log 3\) in every case. Dropping the linear normalization changes the ordinate at 512 by more than 125.75, much larger than the value of the genuine ordinate itself. An affine term disappears from many curvature diagnostics, so it is quite possible for an interpolation test to pass on a function with the wrong normalization. Testing the divisor identity, the affine constant and the one-sided comparisons separately addresses different errors.

\subsection{How a power margin becomes distance}
Let \(1/2<\sigma<\theta<1\). Suppose \(F\) is now fixed and a sufficiently late certified ordinate satisfies \(G_F(t)\le t^\sigma\). For every sufficiently large such \(t\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:collar}
 |y-t|\le t^\theta/(4K_F),\quad y\ge1
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad G_F(y)\le y^\theta .
\end{equation}
To see this, choose the threshold so that \(t^\sigma\le t^\theta/4\) and \(t^\theta/(4K_F)\le t/2\). Inside the radius, \(G_F(y)\le t^\theta/2\) and \(y^\theta\ge(t/2)^\theta\ge t^\theta/2\). The threshold depends on the chosen family and exponents. Both quantifiers over \(t,y\) follow that choice. The supplementary statements \texttt{absorb} and \texttt{extend} mechanically check this implication. In using it on the original support, pick the source exponent with slack \emph{before} collecting endpoints. The same family can then be continued outside the host interval even when no host for its neighboring point is available.

In fact any fixed factor strictly less than one can replace \(1/4\) if the threshold may increase: for \(|y-t|\le c\,t^\theta/K_F\), \((y/t)^\theta\to1\) uniformly and \(t^{\sigma-\theta}\to0\). The elementary linear functions rising away from an anchor show why a Lipschitz-only conclusion has this order of magnitude. The class even admits \(g(y)=g(t)+K_F|y-t|\). A sequence of 1,800 exact rational instances checked equality in the one-sided slope charge. A particular arithmetic function offers extra structure, so this adversarial example belongs to the information class only.

For a finite test take the actual \(\chi_4\) function and the ceiling \(0.15\sqrt y\). Given an observed \(G(t)\), the largest symmetric radius for the constant-two cone follows by solving the strictly decreasing inequality \(G(t)+2r\le0.15\sqrt{t-r}\). The worst side is the left: the ceiling is smaller there. Each entry below is rounded down to six decimal places. We bisected with directed square roots and then checked the normalized function on all intervening pieces. A positive local maximum of an affine function divided by \(\sqrt y\) must occur at a boundary: at an interior critical point of positive value its affine slope is positive and the critical point is a minimum.
\begin{center}\begin{tabular}{rrrr}\toprule
Anchor \(t\)& \(G(t)\), approx.&Certified radius&Actual normalized upper bound\\\midrule
256&0.554968&0.920356&0.036980\\
487&2.867028&0.221215&0.129918\\
512&2.426265&0.483123&0.107764\\
4096&3.212142&3.192058&0.050328\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}\end{center}
The smallest radius occurs at the peak inherited from the preceding study. No experiment here identifies any of these ordinates as one of the existential arithmetic hosts. The radii use a proven all-scale slope constant for one character and directly enclosed finite values. Replacing that character with the full positive family produced by the original level would change both the readings and the constant.

\subsection{A benchmark for a two-ended mesh}
Suppose scalar estimates \(G_F(v)\le v^\sigma\) are available at both endpoints of each missing component \([a,b]\), with \(a\) large, \(b\le2a\), and write \(h=b-a\). Let \(A=\sum_i a_i\), and let \(P\) be the weight of the principal terms. The adverse slope jumps at integer ordinates have total magnitude at most \(A(h+1)(\log b)/a\), since \(|\chi_i(n)|\le1\). The smooth term has second derivative \(-P/y\). The chord formula in the earlier experiment therefore bounds the excursion above the chord by
\[
 \frac{A h(h+1)\log b}{4a}+\frac{P h^2}{8a}.
\]
Here the tent has maximum at most \(h/4\), and its integral as a function of the source ordinate is \((y-a)(b-y)/2\le h^2/8\). Endpoint values \(\le v^\sigma\) give chord \(\le y^\sigma\) by concavity for \(0<\sigma<1\). For a sufficiently small constant depending only on \(A,P\), the spacing condition
\begin{equation}\label{eq:spacing}
 h\le c_F\frac{a^{(1+\theta)/2}}{\sqrt{\log(2a)}}
\end{equation}
makes the adverse excursion at most \(a^\theta/2\) uniformly for late \(a\). Indeed, substitution controls the quadratic term by a constant times \(c_F^2 a^\theta\), and the linear term is of lower order. Absorbing \(y^\sigma\le y^\theta/2\) gives the desired estimate throughout. If a whole cofinal sequence of windows is covered this way, one may take \(R(y)=y^\theta\) there: its weighted integral is just the log length of the window, which is below every fixed positive power eventually.

There is a genuine gain in the sufficient spacing from two supplied ends. Its exponent exceeds \(\theta\) by \((1-\theta)/2\). Both spacings nevertheless have order \(o(a)\). An estimate allowing a hole from \(a\) to a fixed power greater than one of \(a\) cannot ensure either spacing. Moreover, this sufficient condition discards positive curvature and the true distribution of adverse primes. The exact character experiment measures how large that information loss can be when a mesh actually is provided; it cannot populate a mesh at uncomputed arithmetic scales.


\section{What endpoint observations can certify on a genuine prime sum}
\label{sec:endmeasurement}
Suppose a proof has certified the value of a real function at two scales, with all the arithmetic data required to justify each estimate. What can those endpoints buy inside the intervening interval? Even a finite answer depends on the information returned by a successful endpoint check. A measured value, such as \(G(256)/16=0.034685527\ldots\), is much more specific than a successful test of \(G(256)/16\le1\). A certificate that uses the measured value cannot automatically be reproduced by a host that delivers only the upper comparison. We investigate both observation rules on the same real function and the same interval. They give different answers even about \emph{which} prime powers one should sample.

Use the primitive real character \(\chi_4\) modulo four and the fixed normalization
\[
 G(y)=c_4 y+\sum_{n\le y}\frac{\chi_4(n)\Lambda(n)}{n}(y-n),
 \qquad c_4=\frac{L'(1,\chi_4)}{L(1,\chi_4)} .
\]
The sum here is the actual corrected ramp. Its normalized ordinate is \(G(y)/\sqrt y\). On the closed interval \(I=[256,512]\) define the positive mass \(M(f)=\int_I [f(y)]_+ y^{-3/2}\,dy\) and the excess mass \(M_C(f)=\int_I[f(y)-C\sqrt y]_+y^{-3/2}\,dy\). Both are monotone in \(f\). A bound for \(M_C\) is relevant even when pointwise comparison fails, since \(M(f)\le C\log2+M_C(f)\) for \(C\ge0\). The original analytic criterion needs cofinal windows; this one fixed interval addresses the information in endpoint certificates, separate from their arithmetic supply.

The pre-existing experiment located the largest square-root-normalized ordinate at \(487\) on the larger interval \([256,4096]\). Its measurements informed our choice of the first dyadic subinterval and the positive-mass targets. We pinned its cell construction, outward elementary real arithmetic, alternating normalization cross-check and independent prime-power enumerations. Our new computations use that normalization and hold its uncertainty throughout. We obtain \(M(G)\in[0.02249737515340,0.02249737515341]\). This is a held-out reference for the performance comparisons. It is not an input to the endpoint interpolant. The finite decisions below apply to all real ordinates of each tested interval, including the subintervals between primes. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/5396aa19afa70189268f80237c7897cdce426e24/research/uriel/experiments/exceptional-band-mass/evidence/primes/bands.py}{[Pinned character and finite-window implementation]}

\subsection{A sharp envelope using only downward curvature}
Write \(j_n=\chi_4(n)\Lambda(n)/n\). In the open interval there are 23 negative slope jumps and 23 positive ones. An even prime power has zero character weight here. Make a deliberate information cut: keep the positions and magnitudes \(d_n=-j_n>0\) of the downward jumps and set
\[
 \phi(y)=-\sum_{\substack{256<n<512\\ j_n<0}}d_n(y-n)_+.
\]
Then \(V=G-\phi\) is convex on \(I\). Affine normalization and any jumps before the left endpoint are absorbed into the affine part of \(V\). Our observation class is all continuous \(f\) on \(I\) with \(f-\phi\) convex. This class includes the genuine function. It also includes other curves, whose upward curvature can occur at any real location, with other linear normalizations. Worst cases in this class are a way to test what follows from the retained data. They make no claim about which curves can be realized by Dirichlet characters. The positive jump locations used to \emph{choose} a menu later are not imposed as additional constraints on this adversarial class.

For a mesh \(x_0<\cdots<x_m\) including the two ends, first assume the actual values \(G(x_i)\) are known. On \(x_i\le y\le x_{i+1}\) let \(T_i V\) denote the chord between the two values of \(V\). Convexity shows
\[
                     G(y)\le U(y)=\phi(y)+T_i V(y).
\]
This envelope is simultaneously attainable on all cells. The consecutive secant slopes of a convex function are nondecreasing; hence the piecewise chord interpolant of \(V\) is globally convex. Subtracting \(\phi\) from \(U\) gives precisely that interpolant. Thus \(U\) is itself consistent with every observation and dominates every consistent \(f\). Its integral \(M(U)\) is the exact worst admissible mass in the ideal exact-value model. The computer returns outward bounds on that ideal number because the true endpoint values are enclosed, rather than exactly known as rationals.

There is an equivalent expression for what was lost. On one interval \([a,b]\) define the nonnegative tent
\[
 K_{a,b}(y,n)=\frac{(\min(y,n)-a)(b-\max(y,n))}{b-a},
 \qquad a\le n,y\le b .
\]
The difference between the chord of a unit ramp and that ramp is exactly \(K_{a,b}\). Therefore \(U(y)-G(y)=\sum_{a<n<b}[j_n]_+K_{a,b}(y,n)\). The \emph{upward} jumps are the source of information loss after the downward curvature has been kept. If the mesh contains every positive jump, the reconstructed envelope coincides with the entire genuine curve. Conversely an unsampled interior positive jump makes the difference strictly positive inside its cell, regardless of other positive jumps there. It follows that the two ends and the 23 upward jumps are necessary and sufficient for \emph{pointwise reconstruction by this envelope} on this interval. Strict increase of a signed function need not increase its positive mass on a cell where the entire envelope stays negative. A question about exact mass alone is consequently different from pointwise reconstruction.

Three checks isolate the sign in the identity. On \([486,488]\) the only jump is downward at the prime \(487\), so the reconstruction using the two ends and this downward jump is exact. On \([508,510]\) there is a positive jump at \(509\); its omission raises the upper curve at the midpoint by \(\log(509)/(2\cdot509)=0.00612224756046\ldots\). The positive mass of the upper curve there is approximately \(0.000435529936\), while the genuine mass is approximately \(0.000434996804\). On \([488,490]\) there are no effective jumps, and the two curves agree. These controls concern the same real signal as the wider experiment, and the constant \(c_4\) cancels in their pointwise differences.

\subsection{How many observations buy a specified upper mass?}
We fixed two endpoint menus before running the search. The first has the 33 integer locations \(256,264,\ldots,512\). The second has the two ends and the 23 upward jumps of the character in between. Both menus are defined from the coordinate scale or the prime coefficients. Within either menu, the best \emph{subset} for a prescribed number of observations is selected retrospectively after measuring all the candidate endpoints. The resulting subset is an auditable witness that can be rechecked using only the listed observations and the downward jumps. Its discovery required access to the larger menu. We separately test rules that select endpoints without reading candidate values.

For every ordered pair of menu nodes \(i<j\), we compute the mass \(c_{ij}\) of their single-interval envelope, splitting at its downward jumps. On each resulting affine piece \(v(y)=my+d\), locate the positive interval from its endpoint signs and its rational zero when working with rational bracketing curves. The weighted positive integral has primitive \(2m\sqrt y-2d/\sqrt y\) on that interval. For the lower calculation we use a chord through lower endpoint enclosures of \(v\); the upper uses its upper endpoint enclosures. The square roots are evaluated by outward integer square roots on a grid with denominator \(2^{160}\). Refining a mesh decreases or preserves its envelope pointwise. Positive-part clipping is performed before integration and cannot reverse the ordering. Eligibility in this first optimization allows all observations, including values above a proposed pointwise ceiling, because the raw mass objective consumes the numerical values themselves.

Additivity of the mass across consecutive cells gives a finite recurrence. For the optimum \(D(k,j)\) using \(k\) observations including both ends of the path from \(x_0\) to \(x_j\), set \(D(1,0)=0\) and
\[
 D(k,j)=\min_{0\le i<j}\{D(k-1,i)+c_{ij}\},
\]
considering only attainable previous states. Both endpoint observations are counted; \((k-1)\) cells use \(k\) measured values. The proof is by deleting or appending the last cell. The same predecessor must supply both the last intermediate location and the previous path. An unrelated best cell added to an independently optimized partial path would not establish a consistent partition. Applying the recurrence separately to directed lower and upper costs gives a lower bound over all paths and an actual path witnessing the upper bound. For each claimed minimum number of observations the earlier lower bounds exclude the target and the witness clears it. An exhaustive enumeration of all 128 subsets of the seven interior sites of a coarser nine-node grid gives the same two bounds as the recurrence, including all feasible cardinalities.

\begin{table}[htbp]\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule
Requested upper mass & Minimum observations, step-eight menu & Upward-jump menu\\ \midrule
\(0.030\) & 7 & 7\\
\(0.026\) & 8 & 8\\
\(0.024\) & 10 & 9\\
\(1.50\,M(G)\) & 6 & 6\\
\(1.25\,M(G)\) & 7 & 7\\
\(1.10\,M(G)\) & 9 & 9\\
\(1.05\,M(G)\) & 12 & 11\\
\(1.01\,M(G)\) & 21 & 16\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Exact finite cardinality decisions for the endpoint-value upper envelope. The relative rows ask how closely the certificate approaches a previously known full integral. The absolute-budget rows can be checked without supplying that full integral.}
\end{table}

For example, on the first menu the seven nodes \(256,280,312,352,400,448,512\) give an upper mass below \(0.02687496565597\). Every six-node path has upper-model mass at least \(0.03054238754328\). Thus seven suffice for \(0.030\). On the jump menu the nine-node witness \(256,277,313,349,373,397,421,457,512\) gives an upper mass below \(0.02399989901553\). The upper margin beneath \(0.024\) is about \(1.01\times10^{-7}\). Its size makes numerical rounding material to this particular decision. The eight-node lower bound is \(0.02487736152417\). Both thresholds were specified before the search, but the winning endpoints were found in the search.

Two exterior values allow an upper mass \(13.2130581\) times the genuine mass. On the step-eight menu, optimum ratios with 5, 9 and 17 readings are approximately \(1.65662,1.08869,1.01723\), respectively. Equidistant sampling with the same counts gives \(1.76698,1.14531,1.02726\). A blind alternative inserts the location producing the greatest decrease in a conservative tent-area score for \emph{unit} upward jumps. It uses prime-power locations but never reads \(G\) to place a sample. It gives ratios \(1.68905,1.09155,1.02894\) for the same counts. In the last case even the uniform choice is slightly better. Geometry at a kink is informative, yet the signed heights and the zero clipping determine which improvement in the envelope pays off in the positive integral. Ratios represent certificates on this fixed band. They are neither success probabilities nor expected sample complexities for a proof at new scales.


\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\bandfigure{.98\linewidth}{mass}\caption{Optimizing the positions of a fixed number of readings yields an upper-mass certificate. The vertical scale is logarithmic. Each reported optimum is selected from its own finite menu; the endpoint positions on the two curves need not agree.}\end{figure}

\subsection{How accurate must an endpoint reading be?}
One-sided arguments rarely return exact real values. Between a binary test and an exact observation sits a useful family of certified upper readings with known errors. Suppose that a selected finite path was studied with exact ordinates, but each reported upper reading can exceed the true value by at most \(\tau\sqrt{x_i}\). On the cell \([a,b]\), the chord used for the numerical certificate increases by at most the chord of \(\tau\sqrt{\cdot}\), hence by at most \(\tau\sqrt y\). The fixed downward part \(\phi\) is unaffected. Even if these raised ordinates cease to be convex after subtracting \(\phi\), their independent cell chords still dominate every compatible residual curve. For upper bounds this is sufficient. Replacing those chords by the global convex minorant can only improve the answer. The pointwise difference between positive parts is bounded by the upward displacement of the envelope. Integrating, the mass certificate can worsen by at most \(\tau\log(b/a)\) on the cell, or \(\tau\log 2\) over this full mesh. The bound adds log widths across adjacent cells and does not charge the maximum error once per reading.

This gives an interpretation of the two witness margins. The nine-node jump-menu witness for \(0.024\) has less than \(1.02\times10^{-7}\) of headroom in its mass bound; a uniform normalized reporting error of \(10^{-7}\) is small enough to preserve the target, since \(\log2<0.7\). For the seven-node step-eight witness at \(0.030\), even an error of \(0.004\) in each normalized reading still fits by the same estimate. These are explicit sufficient accuracy conditions, with no assumption of statistical cancellation between endpoint errors. A source theorem that merely says the upper ceiling holds does not promise either reporting tolerance around the true height. Conversely, an interval computation with directed rounding does deliver such tolerances, many orders of magnitude smaller here. This distinction explains how an algorithmically small sampling schedule can be mathematically irrelevant to a proof that provides only coarse upper readings, even when both deal with exactly the same observable and interval.

\section{One-sided endpoint certificates obey a different rule}
\label{sec:onesidednumeric}
The upper-value problem is closer to the output of a scalar arithmetic estimate. Suppose that each selected position produces only the assertion \(G(x_i)\le C\sqrt{x_i}\). We cannot use the additional headroom between that upper limit and the true value at a node. First consider the observed sizes on the two outer endpoints. Their normalized values are within \(10^{-12}\) of \(0.034685527196\) and \(0.107226762798\). Both pass the ceiling \(0.12\), but at \(487\) the height is approximately \(0.129917429491\). More importantly for the mass comparison, the true excess mass \(M_{0.12}(G)\) lies in \([0.00037917272386,0.00037917278236]\). At \(0.14\) both endpoints pass again and the genuine excess is exactly zero. Keeping the two \emph{exact} observed values plus downward curvature still allows excess greater than \(0.215349293102\) at \(0.12\) and \(0.202274263153\) at \(0.14\). Reducing exact observations to binary passing tests cannot improve those maximum admissible excesses.

For the two lower tested ceilings \(0.08\) and \(0.10\), the right exterior endpoint fails. The true excess masses are approximately \(0.00449242294\) and \(0.00211221215\). These rows answer a measurement question. They do not qualify as examples where both endpoint certificates passed. To certify the threshold integrals numerically, split at each quarter integer so that both the held-out function and the upper model are affine before subtracting \(C\sqrt y\). The threshold-adjusted piece has second derivative \(C/(4y^{3/2})\ge0\). Its chord is an upper bound; subtracting \(C h^2/(32x^{3/2})\) on \([x,x+h]\) gives a lower bound by the chord error estimate. Apply the outward affine primitive to both and sum. A reported upper zero in the genuine \(0.14\) case results from the sign bounds.

\subsection{The best model consistent with passing tests}
With one-sided data, \(V=G-\phi\) is convex and satisfies point constraints \(V(x_i)\le C\sqrt{x_i}-\phi(x_i)\). The greatest possible convex \(V\) below those finitely many caps, on their convex hull, is the lower convex polygonal hull of the cap points. Indeed, convexity bounds \(V(y)\) above by every chord between two cap points bracketing \(y\). The lower hull consists at each interior point of one such chord and is itself a convex feasible function. Adding \(\phi\) therefore produces the exact worst admissible curve. Some passing tests can have no effect because their cap lies above a chord forced by other tests. This is why concatenating independently saturated cap chords need not be globally feasible. The exact-value calculation avoided this issue because its residual observations were the ordinates of an actual convex function.

We build two hulls for numerical checks, one using downward-rounded caps, the other using upward-rounded caps. Monotonicity of the greatest convex minorant brackets the model for exact caps. The active vertices in these two hulls agree on every tested menu. For the 33 step-eight sites, all pass both \(C=0.14\) and \(C=1\). The upper model still has positive excess at both ceilings. At \(0.14\) its excess lies between \(0.0002649899432\) and \(0.0002649908817\). At \(1\) the range is \(0.0002610557034\) to \(0.0002610624064\). Even 33 passing tests therefore fail to imply complete pointwise control in this precisely defined information model. The admissible adverse signal is the hull construction itself, and can depend on the tested ceiling. A different question would ask whether these positive but small allowances already fit an integral budget.

\subsection{Which observations are indispensable?}
For a passing threshold, there is a simple sufficient mesh. Take the two ends and all interior \emph{downward} jump locations. On every cell of that mesh \(\phi\) is affine, so every admissible \(f\) is convex. If it is bounded at its endpoints by \(C\sqrt y\), its chord is at most the chord of that concave ceiling; hence \(f(y)\le C\sqrt y\) throughout. This sufficiency extends to any concave ceiling for this single nonprincipal character and any continuous observation model with the same downward-curvature upper allowance. A principal character contributes an extra smooth negative curvature. For a family with such a contribution one must account for it separately before using this argument.

There is a matching necessity test. Let \(n\) be an unsampled downward jump. Consider any two sampled positions \(a<n<b\) and the cap chord for the convex residual. At \(n\), after restoring \(\phi\), this chord exceeds \(C\sqrt n\) by at least the positive tent \(d_n K_{a,b}(n,n)\) minus the concavity deficit of the ceiling. Write \(T\sqrt{\cdot}\) for its straight chord on \([a,b]\). Rationalizing the divided differences gives the exact cancellation
\[
 \frac{\sqrt n-T\sqrt{\cdot}(n)}{K_{a,b}(n,n)}
  =\frac{1}{\sqrt n+\sqrt a}-\frac{1}{\sqrt n+\sqrt b}.
\]
The right side is largest when \(a=256\) and \(b=512\). If
\[
 d_n>C\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt n+16}-\frac{1}{\sqrt n+\sqrt{512}}\right),
\]
then \emph{every} bracketing cap chord gives a value strictly above the ceiling at \(n\). Taking the minimum over finitely many such chords leaves a strict excess. The resulting greatest convex minorant is admissible and continuous, so its excess persists on a real neighborhood. This proves that \(n\) is indispensable for enforcing the ceiling using any finite set of passing tests. Arbitrarily dense finite sampling away from \(n\) may make the excess small, but cannot make it vanish. This argument fixes the two exterior endpoints and allows additional points at arbitrary real locations inside the measurement window.

For the 23 downward jumps the least ratio of \(d_n\) to the parenthesized quantity occurs at \(343=7^3\). Directed arithmetic puts the ratio in \([1.215916430058,1.215916430059]\), strictly below the other 22 ratios. This minimum is a sufficient range for the necessity test at \emph{every} atom; we do not identify it as an if-and-only-if transition for all larger ceilings. At both \(C=0.14\) and \(C=1\), every required downward point actually passes the real-character check. Exactly 25 observations, counting the two exterior endpoints, are therefore necessary and sufficient for complete ceiling control in the relaxed class on this window. At \(0.12\) five downward knots fail, including \(487\), and the proposed pointwise conclusion is false even for the genuine function. Testing the other eligible downward knots alone leaves strictly positive worst admissible excess. These conclusions are independent of the spacing of the primary step-eight menu.

The reversal in the roles of upward and downward jumps follows directly from what is being certified. For exact values, the downward curvature is known and the unsampled upward curvature is what separates the true function from its reconstructed upper envelope. For mere caps, a jump at a downward knot can make an otherwise admissible model overshoot the ceiling between two arbitrarily close observations, unless those observations carry additional headroom or slope information. Acquiring a passing certificate at that knot breaks the tent in two. Thus even under the same prime-power formula the geometry of the best data collection changes when the arithmetic conclusion changes. This finite example quantifies the cost of losing information between a numerical observation and a theorem's one-sided output. No minimum number computed here is a lower bound on the number of actual hosts a different arithmetic proof must construct. Such a proof can use information outside the retained model, and in an integral argument it may tolerate a nonzero excess.

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\bandfigure{.98\linewidth}{placement}\caption{Two passing observation menus on \([256,512]\) at ceiling \(C=1\). Placing readings at every downward jump supplies sufficient one-sided information to control the continuous curve. The uniform grid permits positive excess in its best possible worst-case certificate.}\end{figure}



\section{What the endpoint supply settles}
\subsection{A decision by the kind of certificate available}
For the original existential analytic criterion the answer is positive. In the principal conductor-one case the complete square interval \([Y,Y^2]\) is attained; for every character the general result reaches every fixed power strictly between one and two. A single carefully marked arithmetic family contains late intervals of genuine local data, and the comparison controls the same scalar observable on them past a common threshold. Both endpoints and all intervening points are available on any such interval. Recovering the group-order cap explains why the exact square can be attained at conductor one. The near-square widths in general explain how much freedom the proof had when it selected the exponent \(3/2\). Changing that exponent improves our description of the arithmetic supply. The published proof already had a window long enough for its analytic application.

For an interval specified before the arithmetic choice, the answer is conditional. First check whether the desired interval lies in the recovered support of one selection. If it does, the comparison applies directly. If its two ends separately meet a common frozen active-weight test, check the strict floor crossover and all the other range and pair conditions. When those hypotheses hold, the field construction works at all interior points. If only scalar values have been supplied, use a cone or a two-ended interpolation estimate. The observed excess at \(0.12\sqrt y\) rules out an unconditional inference from a pair of low readings.

For \(J>4\) the abstract outer supply can leave gaps of positive log-scale width. The field crossover does not automatically apply across different pairs or different lists. The independent scalar extension is sublinear in ordinary distance. The unresolved case is consequently a prespecified growing gap outside the recovered blocks for which neither a common arithmetic certificate nor a fine scalar mesh has been supplied. The extremizing schedule is consistent with the raw interface. We have not constructed such a gap for an actual arithmetic family.

\subsection{Two experiments that could resolve more}
One route is to track the \emph{same} pair and lists in two successive outer queries and prove compatibility on their overlap. This requires a statement about exceptional integers as the outer field cutoff changes. A bound on their number at each scale allows them to move. A decisive result would be an inclusion or a common controlling list for overlapping ranges, with its rank and discriminant bounds valid on both. The field result here gives the exact inequality to test if a common list can be found.

The other route retains only the fixed observable. Equation~\eqref{eq:spacing} gives one spacing that suffices for two-sided scalar interpolation. A local bound on total adverse jump mass could relax this spacing. For the modulo-four character on our finite interval, all 23 adverse jumps can be listed. At growing scales one would need a theorem about the fixed family with quantitative dependence on scale. A practical next question is whether the arithmetic estimates retain the signs of the contributions or take absolute values before that distinction is available. A source trace can settle that narrower question even when individual cohomological centers remain inaccessible.

These directions distinguish two obstacles that looked alike in the preceding mass experiment. In one, the missing datum is arithmetic identity across scales. In the other, it is the shape of an identified curve between sparse measurements. The sharp examples exploit different freedoms. Advancing either claim requires information in the corresponding class.

\section{Verification and reproduction}
The four primary protocols were recorded locally before their respective new runs. Their first experiment-branch commit archives those texts. The gap calculation and ceiling-only analysis are identified separately in the evidence. The modulo-four interval and its approximate peak were known from earlier experiments. This deliberately difficult test of an endpoint inference uses deterministic enclosures.

From the expanded package run \texttt{python3 -B evidence/reproduce.py}. This checks file digests and reruns the exact and outward-rounded controls. The source audit and Lean supplements have additional input options documented in the README. Receipts record which original objects were authenticated, rebuilt and replayed. The supplementary modules refer to the pinned imports. The pinned repository contains 174,793 Lean source files under its source directory. This experiment rebuilt and replayed the specified dependency subsets. A fresh build of the entire development was infeasible within this run. High-level propagation of a marked domain through the original host and design constructors is a written argument with line anchors. The uniform Lipschitz argument and sharp logarithmic gap theorem are mathematical derivations with separate executable checks. The finite character computations pertain to the stated intervals. Only the combined slope prefix and tail argument yields the all-scale Lipschitz certificate for the modulo-four example.

The manuscript, vector figures and a compressed copy of the evidence directory are in one LuaLaTeX file. Compile with \texttt{latexmk -lualatex} and the manuscript filename. The companion ZIP exposes the same evidence as editable programs and JSON alongside a claim ledger and checksums. Outward real enclosures are used where transcendentals enter. The combinatorial counterexamples use exact arithmetic. The formal checker makes no use of sampled values.
\end{document}
